# State

Tasks may carry `stack: { parent, base, parent_sha, repo, linked, synced_base }`. `parent` is the lower task id, `base` is the PR's current target branch, `parent_sha` is the dependency head used for dispatch or the last synchronization, `repo` is the same GitHub repository as the project, and `synced_base` is the last observed main commit. `linked` records successful `gh stack link`. One unmerged dependency chain forms one stack; outstanding sibling branches are not appended to that chain.

`stack_disabled: true` records an unavailable stacks capability, explicit `stack unstack`, or a replacement middle PR awaiting confirmation of its new chain. It selects ordinary merges, which wait for lower merge evidence before retargeting an existing upper PR. An accepted dependency can still use the ordinary main-based dispatch flow when the repository cannot stack; a submitted dependency requires an available chain.

A successful `stack link` clears fallback markers for its linked members and restores the stack merge gate, including its refusal of admin merges.

When claim or worker spawn readiness depends on an existing stack PR, Tower Crane queries `gh api repos/OWNER/REPO/stacks` outside the state lock and applies linked-state changes only if the affected task snapshot is unchanged. The query runs only when a chain holds two or more unmerged PRs, so a merged dependency needs no check. A spawned worker's gh is limited by its agent file and cannot call that endpoint: spawn runs the query for its task before dispatch, and that worker's claim reads the recorded result. Any other claim queries it itself. A confirmed remote chain clears stale disabled markers; a missing remote link is recorded as unlinked for that member's own chain only. An outage marks only members that have a stack record and a PR, so a dependent without a stack record carries no flag; one left by an earlier outage is cleared once its submitted dependency's chain is confirmed. Transient GitHub errors refuse the readiness check without disabling stacks.

When every lower member has audited successful merge evidence, gates use the project base. Once no unmerged member remains above the task, it is treated as an ordinary PR even if its recorded stack is still linked. Task show, status and the board omit that completed dependency chain. Automatic refresh and explicit `stack sync` retire it without calling `gh stack sync`. A middle task with a PR stays tracked while an upper task is open, so its merged lower PRs remain known to sync and merge. Retirement validates the PR head, branch, repository and base, retargets it to the project base, removes `stack` and `stack_disabled`, and emits `stack complete` with the former parent, new base and PR number. Its sha is unchanged. `stack unstack` uses the same retirement when all other members are merged, without asking GitHub to unstack merged PRs. A remaining GitHub stack membership uses the ordinary single-PR merge fallback through the asynchronous API. Rework notes append only to an existing brief; a brief removed during the append is treated as absent.

A child without a PR retires as soon as every lower member has merged, including an unlinked, disabled or running child. The merge refresh or next observation removes its stack metadata and emits `stack complete` with `pr: null`. Worker dispatch rechecks lower PRs before worktree preparation and readiness. A remote merge at each recorded lower head, branch, repository and dependency or project base authorizes retirement before spawn even when its local merge receipt has not arrived. It does not create merge evidence. The parent observations and retirement use the same input snapshot, so a changed chain refuses dispatch. Squash and merge parents use this path even when GitHub has deleted their branches.

The automatic retirement note lives in `task.notes`. It does not emit a `task note` event or create an `owner-comment` notification when the owner triggers retirement.

A middle member with an open task above keeps its stack lineage, but moves its PR and stored `stack.base` to the project base once every lower member has merged. It uses the same PR validation and closed-PR replacement as retirement, including when the stack is disabled or unlinked. `stack base` records `{parent, previous_base, base, pr}` and adds the automatic merge-base note when the base or PR changes. A replacement marks the retained member and its upper members unlinked until their new PR chain is confirmed; the retained member selects ordinary merge fallback in the meantime. The upper members keep their parent task ids and dependency branches.

Dispatch applies this base change after a verified remote merge even before local merge evidence arrives. Its completed lower chain needs no dispatch-time `gh stack sync`; a sync must not restore the former dependency base. A missing task checkout resumes from its own head and preserves retained lineage on the project base.

A failed PR retarget keeps the stack record and fallback flag for retry. While that record remains and every lower task has merged, the ordinary merge fallback may retarget the recorded dependency base to the project base. After retirement removes the record, only the project base is valid; moving the PR back to the former dependency branch is refused without a PR write.

Retirement preserves the child branch and commits. A task note tells the worker to merge the project base before continuing work or opening its PR and to resolve conflicts while preserving its commits. If GitHub closed the dependent PR while deleting its recorded base, retirement finds an open PR from the same branch against the project base or creates one with the closed PR's title and body. The replacement must match the submitted head, branch and repository and have auto-merge disabled. The task records its new PR number in the same write as `stack complete`; a separate `stack pr replaced` event records the former PR, replacement PR, sha and new revision. Replacement bumps the revision and returns an accepted task to submitted, so gates and review must run for the new PR. A closed PR against an unrelated base, a moved head or a failed GitHub call leaves the stack intact for inspection or retry.

The shared generated-conflict preflight runs before automatic refresh from
merge, worktree preparation and spawn. It can also retire a linked dependent
whose lower members all have confirmed merge evidence. It uses the same
snapshot and PR validation as ordinary completed-chain retirement, emitting
`stack complete` before generated repair. This prevents synchronization from
requesting worker rework first. Generated-only conflicts receive a new
submitted head; mixed conflicts retain a prepared merge; unconfirmed remote
conflicts wait. Other stacks keep their existing automatic sync behavior.

`github_stack` holds the unmodified `pull_request.stack` object (or the payload's top-level `stack`) from a trusted payload passed to `stack webhook`, or null when the payload has no stack. The repository and PR number must match a known task. Task show and board sheets expose this metadata separately from Tower Crane's local dependency chain. Webhook metadata does not satisfy a gate, change a submitted head, accept a task or prove a merge.

Stack merges record ordinary `merge` evidence and matching gate audit events for each confirmed task at its own submitted sha and revision. Their command receipts include the remote membership query, every PR head check, bottom-up asynchronous merge requests (`gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{n}/merge-async --method POST` with `merge_method=merge` and `expected_head_sha`) and their status polls, upper PR retargeting and the confirmations. Merge commits keep every accepted head as an ancestor of the project base; the submitted shas remain unchanged. A refused or queued member stops later merges; confirmed accepted lower members retain successful evidence even when the target fails. Terminal POST or job failures retain their reason across status and PR read errors. Unless the PR is confirmed merged at its accepted head, the failure is reported when the PR is read open or when the confirmation deadline expires. Branches are retained while merging a linked chain. Sync emits `stack sync` events for the affected tasks with `ok`, `head`, the parsed abort `reason`, and `failure` (`conflict`, `tool`, or null on success). A moved branch or an explicit conflict report emits rework and appends its reason to the task's brief. A tooling or transport failure preserves unchanged submissions, claims, briefs, gate evidence and stack metadata; the next refresh retries it. A branch moved before a failed push still requires rework. Refresh deferred for a live worker, a dirty worktree or a member worktree with a stopped rebase, am, merge, cherry-pick, revert or bisect emits `stack sync deferred`; it does not change the claim. A worktree that such an operation detached is found through the branch it started from, and dispatch of that branch's task refuses with the same reason. A spawn inside `wait` whose stack refresh a drain stopped also emits `stack sync deferred` for its task and starts no agent, and the rest of that refill batch waits; the next wait dispatches them. PR linking uses `stack dispatch`, `stack link`, `stack readiness`, `stack unavailable` and `stack link failed` events. A link applies only when what it read is unchanged under the lock: the project settings and, for each member of the chain, its status, head, branch, PR, stack record, `stack_disabled` flag and merge state. Other writes to the members, such as gate evidence, notes, usage samples and reviewer dispatches and exits, do not refuse it. A link refused because its inputs changed during its GitHub calls is retried from fresh state up to five times in the same observer pass; only then is `stack link failed` recorded, and the next observer pass retries. An open PR whose head GitHub moved after a lower stack merge, the requested task or a dependent above it, records a `stack head` event with its old and new sha and returns to submitted.

Sync holds the state lock only for its initial read and final compare-and-apply. Its gh commands and branch reads, fetches and pushes run unlocked. Application compares project configuration, stack membership, complete member task records and member events against the snapshot, leaving out hook, startup and spawn session receipts, automation reservation receipts and the orchestrator notice a hook stop writes. A difference refuses application without writing sync events, briefs or task records. Writes to unrelated tasks are preserved by applying to freshly read state.

Linking, completed-chain retirement and unstacking use the same snapshot, unlocked commands and compare-and-apply rule. Automatic linking runs after exit collection commits, outside that mutation. A stale link or unstack result cannot clear a new claim or replace member metadata. Remote PR changes may already have completed when application is refused; inspect them before retrying.

The shared command runner rejects Git and gh calls inside state mutation transactions. Spawn prepares repository directories and credentials before its write; acceptance and review dispatch prepare local CI inputs and snapshots before their writes and reject changed inputs. View rendering starts after the mutation releases its lock and reads current state.

Hosted CI runs on every stack member at its own base. `ci.yml` has no base filter on pull requests, so a stacked task's PR runs the `ci.yml` jobs on any task branch, and its required runs report before its base merges; pushes to `main` run them too.

Tests, cleanup, review diffs and local CI use the dependency branch as the base for stacks with unmerged lower members. Once all lower members have merge evidence, those checks use the project base. Old head evidence cannot count after the worker resubmits a refreshed head. Stack merge checks all lower heads first, then pins each member's accepted full head with the asynchronous merge API because GitHub's stack merge command has no head-match option; see cli.md for partial completion and the ordinary admin fallback.

Tower Crane keeps a project's plan and progress in plain files. People and dashboards read them; the `tower-crane` CLI and serve's owner forms write through the same locked, validated functions. An agent that needs to change state runs a command.

## Where

The state directory is the first of:

1. `--state DIR` on the command line
2. `TOWER_CRANE_STATE` in the environment (`tower-crane spawn` sets it for every agent it starts)
3. `.tower-crane/` at the root of the main checkout, found through `git rev-parse --git-common-dir`, so every worktree of the repo shares one state

`tower-crane init` adds `.tower-crane/` to `.git/info/exclude`. State is not committed unless the project chooses to. In a bare repository, `.tower-crane/` sits inside the common git directory.

Commands that need the repository (`worktree`, `spawn`, the gates) use the one Tower Crane runs in; outside a repository, the one that holds the state directory.

## Agent identity

The identity recorded in state and events comes from `--agent NAME`, then `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT`. With neither, the CLI uses `owner` only when stdin and stdout are both TTYs and `TOWER_CRANE_TASK` is unset. In every other case it exits 2 with `no agent: pass --agent NAME or set TOWER_CRANE_AGENT`, before reading or writing state. An empty or whitespace-only identity exits 2 with the same message. Help needs no identity.

Resolving either identity source to `owner` (the terminal fallback included) is refused unless the owner runs the process, and the refusal starts with `owner identity needs a process the owner runs (docs/state.md#agent-identity)` and names the rule that failed:

- `TOWER_CRANE_TASK` is set: a task process never acts as owner.
- `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT` names any identity other than `owner`: a process started as another identity, such as `orchestrator` or a spawned `worker-T1-1`, never acts as owner, whatever `--agent` it passes.
- stdin or stdout is not a terminal, and `TOWER_CRANE_OWNER_KEY` does not hold the owner key. The key is the owner's own credential for scripts and other non-interactive owner commands: `tower-crane owner-key --agent owner`, run at a terminal, creates it (mode 600, in a mode 700 directory) as `owner/key` under the canonical `owner_config_dir` recorded in `project.json` (`~/.config/tower-crane/owner/key` by default) and prints its path, never the key. Every spawn creates that directory and hides it from claude's and codex's sandboxes, and from claude's Read, Grep and Glob tools, so no sandboxed agent reads the key. The CLI captures `TOWER_CRANE_OWNER_KEY` privately and removes it from its environment at command entry, before state discovery or authentication can start a child. Nothing it starts, including Git used to discover the state directory, agents or gates, inherits the key. Terminal checks leave piped stdin unchanged for hooks and command input. An unsandboxed process running as the same user can read the user's files, the key included; the key keeps owner identity out of reach of `--agent owner` alone, not of a process that deliberately reads the owner's secrets.

`spawn` passes the name `<job>-<task>-<n>` (`worker-T1-1`, `reviewer-T1-1`) in `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT`. A sandboxed claude or codex agent cannot write the state files (a codex agent's commands write only its own `HOME`, sessions and broker directory there); its state changes go through its spawn's [state broker](ladder.md#state-broker), which records them under the spawned name whatever the agent passes, allows only its role's commands on its own task, and refuses another identity. Its credential is the token in its private broker directory, made for that spawn and invalid once the agent exits. Its prompt says `you are not the owner; never pass --agent owner` and closes with `run tower-crane with --agent <name> if TOWER_CRANE_AGENT is missing`, so a shell that loses the environment can still record the correct identity.

A spawned harness can run a command before its dispatch records the spawn, and a spawned identity's role comes from that record. The supervisor passes each launch `TOWER_CRANE_STARTUP_DEADLINE`, its launch time plus the startup window (10 s) in epoch milliseconds. A command that carries it, runs as `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT` against the `TOWER_CRANE_STATE` it names, and finds no spawn record for that identity waits at entry until the record appears or the deadline passes, then resolves its role. `owner` and the plain `orchestrator` name, which engine work uses, never wait. An orchestrator's first `wait` therefore registers as a controller even when it starts before its dispatch commits.

The credential location for an initialized project comes only from `project.json`, never from the command's `TOWER_CRANE_CONFIG`, `HOME` or `USERPROFILE`. Changing those variables does not redirect authentication or a pinned sandbox denial. An absent, invalid or missing recorded credential refuses headless owner access. For a project without `owner_config_dir`, only an explicit owner at a terminal can record it with `owner-key`; an existing binding is never replaced. Until it is recorded, spawning still works and hides `owner/` under the spawning environment's resolved Tower Crane config directory, where a terminal `owner-key` would create the key. This denial does not establish a binding or allow owner authentication.

## Orchestrator lease

The first orchestrator write takes `tasks.json.orchestrator_lease` under the state lock. Older state files can omit it; null means released. It holds `{ session_id, harness_session_id, agent, pid, host, heartbeat }`, plus `pidns`, Linux `start_ticks` and `persistent`. The selected harness or session shell records `persistent: true`; a caller fallback records `persistent: false`. `pid` identifies the selected session ancestor or fallback caller, rather than the short-lived CLI process.

The session key binds the host and nearest harness ancestor (`claude`, `codex`, `opencode`, `agy`, `pi`, `gemini`, `aider`, `goose` or `cursor-agent`, including Node and Python entrypoints) or session shell to the first available `CLAUDE_SESSION_ID`, `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, `CODEX_THREAD_ID` or `CODEX_SESSION_ID`. A session shell is a shell that runs neither `-c` nor a script file and is a login shell (`-bash`, the `-zsh` that macOS `ps` prints for a Terminal login shell, `-l` or `--login`, so `bash --posix --login` counts), runs with `-i` or `--interactive`, or, on Linux, has a terminal on stdin. fish's `-C` takes its init command as a value, so `fish -i -C 'source x'` counts. A shell with `-c` is a per-command shell on every platform. Windows command lines are split by CommandLineToArgvW rules, so a backslash-escaped quote stays inside its argument and `"a""b"` is `a"b`; macOS `ps` output is split on spaces. Any other agent CLI started from a session shell is not a session ancestor: it shares that shell's lease, so two such CLIs run from one shell share one identity. Intermediate shells and script files share that ancestor's identity. Without such an ancestor, it keeps the first non-transient caller from the parent process chain and does not release that fallback lease on process exit. Without a harness id it uses the host and selected process identity alone. Linux start ticks and Windows creation ticks identify that process; changes to ancestors above it do not change the key. Linux process start times and pid namespace distinguish reused pids and namespaces. Windows does not reparent orphans, so the ancestor walk stops at a parent whose creation time is later than its child's: that pid was reused by an unrelated process. A different harness process or session id cannot share a live lease, including two live copies of the same resumed harness id. In-process subagents, such as Claude Code's Agent tool, run inside the orchestrator's harness process and share its session id, so they share its lease identity; only `--agent` (or `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT`) separates their role from the orchestrator's.

Windows parent discovery runs before taking the state lock. One CIM process snapshot supplies every ancestor; only the caller's chain is returned from PowerShell. This avoids one provider round trip per ancestor while keeping command-line output within the pipe buffer. The existing 60-second hang guard allows cold PowerShell startup under load. A failed or timed-out snapshot refuses the command without choosing a fallback identity.

Native orchestrator commands renew the matching holder's heartbeat at command entry, including reads, once it is a sixtieth of `lease_minutes` old (one minute by default). The check reads the files without the state lock; only a renewal, acquisition or replacement takes it. A read tries the lock once and never waits: a busy lock leaves the renewal to a later command. A read never takes a vacant lease or renews another session's lease. Writes from another live session exit 1 naming the holder, pid, host, heartbeat and recovery commands. The guard runs before CLI commands start external work and again inside every state mutation. MCP initialization and discovery need no project or lease; actual tool actions use the same guard. Pure inbox reads stay open. `authority`, `bench gates` and `bench tokens` also remain open to competing readers and never acquire a vacant lease. Wait startup tries its lease transaction without blocking behind the state lock, so its event deadline stays effective; any later mutation still checks the lease under the lock. Workers and owner commands keep their existing authority.

Harness session ids in the environment can be copied by unrelated processes, so they do not prove lease identity. Native commands keep the process ancestry rule. A caller carrying the holder's harness session id outside the holder's process tree gets a refusal naming that mismatch as detached, without telling it to release its own lease. A loop launched with `setsid` or `nohup` can leave that tree when its intermediate parent exits; starting a new process group alone does not remove ancestry. Run native commands as children of the holding session, or let `tower-crane wait` and authenticated engine automation run gates for that session. Callers with unrelated session ids retain the existing release, expiry and owner-takeover guidance.

The lease expires after `project.limits.lease_minutes` of idle time, using the existing project lease window (60 minutes by default). After expiry the next orchestrator write replaces it. A persistent holder whose process has exited on the same host (and, on Linux, the same recorded pid namespace) releases the lease at once, so a restarted session does not wait out the idle window; on Linux the recorded start ticks mean a reused pid does not keep it. A fallback holder, or a lease recorded without these fields, still expires by idle time. The persistent holder is the selected harness or session shell. Intermediate scripts and per-command wrappers cannot release its lease by exiting. A holder's exit also ends engine reactions bound to it. `tower-crane orchestrator release` clears the calling holder's lease. Only the owner can run `tower-crane orchestrator takeover --agent owner`, which clears a live holder explicitly; the next orchestrator write acquires it. Both commands record the previous holder in the audit log.

Engine reactions marked `via: automation` bind to the session in the lease record whose process has not exited, including reactions from a monitor or owner waiter. The heartbeat's age does not matter: a reaction that starts after the holder idled out binds to that holder, so the holder's next command, which renews its own heartbeat, does not stop it. Engine work never acquires a lease or renews its heartbeat. Usage collectors and harness hook writers use the same engine provenance, so short-lived helper processes cannot become lease holders. With no lease, or a holder whose process has exited, engine work runs without taking a lease and leaves the lease available for the next native writer; an expired lease stays available too. An active reaction retains its captured binding, including a vacant binding. Release, takeover, a new holder replacing the expired lease, or the holder's exit refuses its later state writes. Fresh engine work can resume under the current holder. This lease coordinates trusted sessions, like the existing state lock; it is not an authentication boundary for processes that can write state files directly.

Engine provenance comes from the process doing the engine work, never from the environment. In-process reactions, collectors and observers mark their own context. A CLI child of an engine helper (the spawn supervisor's usage collector and hook writer, and the harness hook bridge) sets `TOWER_CRANE_VIA=automation` and presents the key from the dispatched agent's home, `homes/<agent>/automation.key`, as the first line of the child's stdin, with `TOWER_CRANE_AUTOMATION_KEY_FD=0` naming that descriptor. The key is never in an environment: Linux keeps a process's initial environment readable in `/proc/<pid>/environ` for its whole life, and the Stop hold's `wait --inbox` lives as long as the orchestrator idles. Each home build writes a new random key with mode `0600` to a staged file beside it and renames it into place, so a helper reading it during a rebuild finds a whole key or none, never an empty or partial one. The CLI reads the key line at startup, before any input the command reads, removes `TOWER_CRANE_AUTOMATION_KEY_FD` from its environment and checks the key against that agent's home. Without a matching key, `TOWER_CRANE_VIA=automation` is ignored: the command runs as its caller's native command, so a second session that sets it is refused like any other. Spawned agents do not inherit `TOWER_CRANE_VIA`, `TOWER_CRANE_AUTOMATION_KEY_FD` or the dispatcher's `CLAUDE_SESSION_ID`, `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, `CODEX_THREAD_ID` and `CODEX_SESSION_ID`, so an agent's lease names its own harness session.

## Authority

The owner hands the run to an orchestrator and is asked only when the owner is required. `lib/authority.js` holds the one table that classifies every guarded setting and command, and one check that every command uses:

- **operational**: the orchestrator or the owner. The orchestrator acts under its own identity; the event records that agent, and `project set`, `ladder set`, `ladder harness` and `ladder save-user` events add `"authority": "orchestrator"`.
- **owner-required**: the owner only. When the orchestrator tries one, the command writes nothing it asked for, opens a decision for the owner and refuses with its id. The decision and its `ask` event carry `escalation: { settings, change }` naming the settings and the requested owner-required values, with options `approve` and `decline`. The same open request reuses that decision. Only the owner answers an escalation, from the CLI or the board. After `approve`, the orchestrator runs the same command again: a request whose owner-required settings and values equal an approved, unapplied escalation is allowed once, and the decision records `applied: { at, by }`. Operational parts of the same command are the orchestrator's either way. `decline`, or the owner making the change, leaves the orchestrator's next attempt to open a new decision. A waiver the orchestrator records under an approval carries `approved_by` with the decision id, and counts only while the log holds that escalation's `ask` and the owner's `approve` answer.
- **refused**: no identity makes the change while the task stands as it is. The command writes nothing and names the step that comes first (`rework`); no decision opens.

Gate command pins and `gates.clean_paths` are operational settings for trusted host gate code. The orchestrator can select that code and the read-only paths its worker invocation needs, including tool installations under the spawning user's home. Declared directories authorize reads of their contents, including data files. The checks for Tower Crane's protected stores still apply. This authority applies to gate tool paths. General `sandbox` grants and ladder reach changes retain owner authority, and workers and reviewers can change neither class.

Harness capabilities are declared once in `lib/agents.js` as `CAPABILITIES`. `lib/authority.js` reads each harness's `sandbox` flag to classify primary, inherited fallback and default-harness moves; it does not infer confinement from private homes or the separate `osSandbox` flag. Opencode has both sandbox capabilities false, so moving a confined role to it requires the owner. `osSandbox` controls verified OS isolation for the state broker and nested Chrome setup; it does not classify ladder reach. New harness renderers change their capability flag; the authority rule and its tests follow it.

The owner can run a project by telling the orchestrator, and every row below has a CLI path: the orchestrator makes operational changes itself and asks only for owner-required ones; the owner approves with `tower-crane answer D<n> --choice approve --agent owner`. A row no command changes, `publish`, is requested with `ask --setting publish`; `ask` without `--question` refuses a row a command changes, since that command applies the approval. From the board (`serve --agent owner`) the owner answers decisions, which approves or declines escalations, and changes the ladder (`/api/ladder`) and tier rungs (`/api/tiers`); those writes go through the same check and audit event with `mode: board`. Board controls for the remaining rows are not built yet (T89 part 2).

Every allowed change of a guarded setting appends one `setting` event in the same write as the command's own event, just before it: `detail: { command, actor, mode, settings, approved_by?, policy? }`; `policy` replaces `approved_by` on a publish the publish policy allowed ([below](#publish-policy)). `actor` is `owner` or `orchestrator`, `mode` is `board` for a write through `serve` and `cli` otherwise, and `settings` maps each guarded setting the command changed to its class. A refused or escalated request records none, and neither does a command that fails after its check: `accept --waive` and `spawn --role orchestrator` apply the owner's approval and audit it only in the write that records the acceptance or the started spawn; unguarded fields such as `name` and `goal` record none.

When the owner's successful write satisfies a request's fields, matching open or approved requests record `applied: { at, by: "owner" }` in that same write. The owner may write additional settings or fields at once; every satisfied request is retired. An open request also becomes answered `approve`, with an owner `answer` event. Failed commands leave these requests untouched, including acceptance that fails a gate and a spawn that never starts. The owner's setting audit uses their own authority, without `approved_by`. A later orchestrator retry needs a new decision, even if the owner has since changed the value again.

Matching preserves the request's target, such as a rung, user file or decision. Ladder/rung objects group fields, so an owner can satisfy an environment request while also writing scope or another rung. Replacement values must match whole: adding keys inside an environment map or routes inside a fallback list does not satisfy a request for a different value. JSON formatting and object key order do not change the value. Orchestrator approval consumption still requires the exact request; only owner-write retirement accepts additional fields.

Budget requests match the owner's written amounts independently of whether those amounts currently raise, lower or repeat the budget. For example, after approving a request to raise hours from 5 to 10, owner writes of 20, 10 and 5 retire that approval at the write of 10. The subsequent orchestrator retry needs another decision. The audit still classifies the owner's decrease or unchanged amount as operational `budget.lower`; operational orchestrator writes do not retire an unused raise approval.

`project set --budget-hours null` and `--budget-tokens null` remove their limits; `task update` accepts the same flags for a task's limits. Removing a finite limit is `budget.raise`, so the owner may do it directly and the orchestrator needs a one-use approval. Task-budget approvals name the task, and a task-budget write cannot retire a project-budget request. Live-budget alerts are owner questions, not setting approvals. `init` accepts the same nullable flags. Other numeric flags retain their numeric-only parsing.

The same rule applies to primary ladder reach grants and task kinds: an owner writing the requested tools, harness, arguments or kind retires the matching request even when the current edit is operational or unchanged. Primary reach requests describe grants, so additional tool or unconfined-route grants in the same owner write are allowed. Only written fields and routes affected by a written harness count; an unrelated model edit does not settle a reach request. Task cancellation and review waivers also retire matching owner requests after their owner-only condition has cleared. Authorization and audits still use the current classes.

Waiver requests include `{ accept, sha, revision, waive, reason }`. Their approval and the recorded waiver must name the same task, SHA and positive revision. Changing acceptance, dependencies or capabilities requires a new approval even when the commit is unchanged. A waiver approval without a revision cannot authorize a new accept or back an orchestrator's waiver evidence; request fresh approval for the current revision.

Release requests include `{ release, holder, claim_since }`, binding the task, holder and claim start time. Renewing that claim keeps the approval usable, but a replacement claim needs a new decision even when the holder has the same name. Browser-kit requests include `{ user_file, servers }`, using the same resolved absolute path the command writes. Changing `TOWER_CRANE_CONFIG` to another target needs another approval, and owner writes retire requests only for their own target file. Legacy approvals without these bindings cannot authorize the corresponding command; request fresh approval.

An interruption audits `task.interrupt` in the write that releases the claim. `task update --interrupt` combines that row with its other guarded changes in one setting event. A mixed waiver request attaches `approved_by` only to owner-required waivers; an operational review waiver retains its own eligibility.

The Controls page (`/controls`) uses this same check and event for every authority row, with `mode: board`. An owner-required change submitted there opens an escalation even for the owner; approving it on the page runs its recorded `request: {command, flags, pos}` through the same command handler as the owner, once. Approving an orchestrator's escalation on the page only answers it: the orchestrator's repeat of the same command uses the approval, as from the CLI. That includes an owner's request the orchestrator then asked for too (`repeat_by`). A publish request is answered the same way. A later save on the page uses only an approval the owner asked for there that no orchestrator repeats; with an orchestrator's approval pending, it opens the owner's own confirmation instead, and saving publish again reports the existing approval without using it. No browser-supplied actor, state path or alternate authority class is accepted. A failed application leaves the approved decision unapplied.

Workers, reviewers and every other identity are refused both. For an operational change they ask the orchestrator (`tower-crane msg --to orchestrator` or a task note), not the owner. An owner-required change they request with `tower-crane ask --question Q --kind setting --setting KEY`, which keeps the decision with the owner. A worker's or reviewer's `ask --kind technical` that names no owner-required setting opens a technical decision the orchestrator can answer; a `spend`, `credential` or `setting` kind stays with the owner (see `decisions.json`).


| Setting | Class | Changed by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `gates.tests_cmd`, `gates.clean_cmd`, `gates.tests_proof_cmd` | operational | `project set --tests-cmd`, `--clean-cmd`, `--tests-proof-cmd`; gate self-pinning below |
| `gates.clean_paths` | operational | `project set --clean-paths`: read-only tool dependency paths for sandboxed agents |
| `gates.executors` | operational | `project set --executors` |
| `gates.tests_timeout_min`, `gates.clean_timeout_min`, `gates.host_probe_timeout_min` | operational | `project set --tests-timeout-min`, `--clean-timeout-min`, `--host-probe-timeout-min` |
| `gates.tmp_root` | operational | `project set --tmp-root` |
| `gates.rework_hold_min` | operational | `project set --rework-hold-min` |
| `gates.priority` | operational | `gates prioritize ID --reason R`: a task's queued submitted gate reactions run before older submitted work; accepted merges stay first |
| `ci.required`, `ci.ignore_apps`, `ci.capped_review` | operational | `project set --ci-required`, `--ci-ignore-apps`, `--ci-capped-review` |
| `ci.local` | operational | `project set --ci-local`, `task update --ci-local` |
| `ci.dispatch` | operational | `project set --ci-dispatch` |
| `tests.paths`, `tests.keep`, `tests.host_only`, `tests.host_env`, `tests.mode`, `tests.by_kind`, `tests.expensive`, `tests.map`, `tests.crucial` | operational | `project set --tests-*`; `tests map refresh` also pins `tests.map` |
| `limits.workers`, `limits.lease_minutes`, `limits.paused` | operational | `project set --workers`, `--lease-minutes`, `--paused REASON` (empty resumes) |
| `resources.tasks_max`, `resources.memory_max_bytes` | operational | `project set --tasks-max N --memory-max-bytes N`; positive integers, per agent or gate including descendants |
| `budget.lower` | operational | `project set` or `task update --budget-hours`, `--budget-tokens` to a lower limit, or a limit where there was none |
| `budget.stop` | operational | stopping a spawn whose live usage crosses a project or task budget; the supervisor does it on the orchestrator's behalf |
| `merge.keep_branch` | operational | `project set --merge-keep-branch` |
| `review` | operational | `project set --review-policy` |
| `governor` | operational | `project set --governor` |
| `ladder.harness`, `ladder.model`, `ladder.profile`, `ladder.provider`, `ladder.effort`, `ladder.args`, `ladder.supervision` | operational, keeping the sandbox | `ladder set`, `ladder harness`; a worker, reviewer or small rung, and each personal fallback route that follows its harness, stays on a sandboxed harness declared in `lib/agents.js`, with args restricted to its allowed flags (`ladder.reach` otherwise) |
| `ladder.tools`, `ladder.mcp` | operational, with two conditions | `ladder set --tools`, `--mcp`: an MCP server the owner's harness config already defines, never a new command, and a harness built-in tool that leaves the rung's sandbox confinement (write paths, env, scope, network) unchanged |
| `ladder.fallbacks` | operational, with each changed route field retaining its class below | `ladder set --fallbacks`, `--clear fallbacks`; writes only personal routes in the user file |
| `decision.delegate` | owner-required | `decision delegate --answerers`, `--technical`; approval binds the target decision and supplied values |
| `ladder.save_user` | operational | `ladder save-user`: writes only the default harness and the primary rungs the project defines to the user file, keeping its personal fallbacks and other keys |
| `ladder.web_mcp` | owner-required | `ladder set research --web-mcp`, `--clear web_mcp`: the explicit web server command and tools |
| `research.min_sources` | owner-required | `project set --research-min-sources` |
| `task.kind` | operational | `task update --kind` to any kind, except leaving `code` (`task.downgrade`) |
| `task.downgrade` | owner-required | `task update --kind` from `code` to `docs`, `research`, `design` or `ops`, which drops the tests and clean gates, at any status |
| `task.kind.submitted` | refused | `task update --kind` on a submitted or accepted task, whoever asks; `rework` the task first |
| `task.tier` | operational | `task update --tier` when the value changes |
| `task.priority` | operational | `task update --priority urgent\|normal` |
| `task.scope_ruling` | operational | `task note --kind scope-ruling` records a design change; `task update --closed-acceptance` declares a changed acceptance list exhaustive after repeated review failures |
| `task.interrupt` | operational | `interrupt ID`, and `task update --interrupt` on a live claim whose requirements change |
| `task.needs_owner` | operational | `owner-done`; `task update --needs-owner` clearing or replacing an existing reason |
| `task.cancel_hold` | operational | `task update --status cancelled` on a task whose `needs_owner` the orchestrator or the engine set (`needs_owner_by.role` is `orchestrator` or `engine`, as for the respawn cap) |
| `task.cancel_needs_owner` | owner-required | `task update --status cancelled` on a task whose `needs_owner` the owner or a worker set, or whose setter is not recorded |
| `task.rerecord_review` | owner-required | `hotfix ID` re-recording a cancelled hotfix whose follow-up a failed independent review opened, or one whose failure is not recorded |
| `waive.review` | operational | `accept --waive review` when the reviewer is capped or down at the submitted head: a `check ci` there recorded a capped review run (`ci.capped_review`, kept as `capped_review` on the evidence), or a review spawn there exited without a verdict |
| `merge.admin` | owner-required | `project set --merge-admin` |
| `decision_delegation` | owner-required | `project set --decision-delegation` |
| `waive.sources` | owner-required | `accept --waive sources` |
| `claim.release` | owner-required | `release` of another agent's claim while its process is live or unverified |
| `sandbox`, `env`, `env_file`, `scope` | owner-required | `project set` or `ladder set`, including on a personal fallback route (`ladder set --fallbacks`); spawn runs a fallback route that sets them, or otherwise carries authority, only when that command wrote it ([below](#fallback-route-grants)) |
| `sandbox.session_bus` | owner-required | `project set --session-bus`: lets claude and codex worker sandboxes reach the user's session bus, which lifts their confinement; the risk is in [project.json](#projectjson) |
| `ladder.command` | owner-required | `ladder set --command`: the program a rung runs |
| `ladder.reach` | owner-required | `ladder set --tools` opting in to a tool that is not a harness built-in (such as a `Bash(...)` rule) or that changes the rung's sandbox: claude `Edit`, `Write`, `NotebookEdit`; codex `memories`, `plugins`, `apps`, `browser_use`, `computer_use`; pi `edit`, `write`; agy file edits and subagents; a `ladder set` or `ladder harness` that moves a worker, reviewer or small rung, or a user-file fallback route that follows its harness, off a sandboxed harness; args on an unsandboxed harness, including agy, opencode and pi despite their restricted flag lists |
| `budget.raise` | owner-required | `project set` or `task update --budget-hours`, `--budget-tokens` to a higher limit or none |
| `delegation` | owner-required | `spawn --role orchestrator`, which hands orchestrator authority to a new agent |
| `waive.review_live` | owner-required | `accept --waive review` when no reviewer is capped or down at the submitted head |
| `waive.tests`, `waive.clean`, `waive.ci` | owner-required | `accept --waive tests`, `clean`, `ci` |
| `browser_kit` | owner-required | `browser-kit set`, the MCP servers browser tasks get in every project |
| `publish.policy` | owner-required | `project set --publish-policy`: the conditions under which the orchestrator publishes without a per-release ask ([below](#publish-policy)) |
| `publish` | owner-required | `ask --setting publish`: anything that publishes outside the repository, such as a release or a package; the orchestrator publishes once the owner approves, or at once while the publish policy holds |

Tools and MCP servers are operational because choosing what a rung works with is running the project, and the owner's autonomy rule hands that to the orchestrator; the two conditions keep it from widening what an agent reaches. An MCP server runs outside the rung's sandbox, so the orchestrator may opt in only a server already defined in the owner's own harness config (claude `mcp.json` or `.claude.json`, codex `config.toml`, agy `~/.gemini/config/mcp_config.json`, opencode JSON/JSONC config and inline `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`); naming another is refused, and the owner adds it. A tool the agent files name (claude tool names, codex features, agy native tool names, pi built-ins) or codex `web_search` re-enables a harness built-in inside the same sandbox; any other tool opens a `ladder.reach` decision. Only opt-ins new to a rung and harness are checked, including rungs a `ladder harness` change moves to another harness. The owner can overrule either condition by making the change. The conditions hold because the rung keeps its sandbox: moving a confined role off a sandboxed harness (including personal fallback routes that follow its harness), or adding args on an unsandboxed harness, opens a `ladder.reach` decision. A route the owner already put there is the orchestrator's to tune with the same args; the orchestrator rung runs unsandboxed, so only args on an unsandboxed harness open a decision there. The sandbox, env, env_file and scope grants, `merge.admin`, `decision_delegation`, budget raises, delegation, the publish policy and publishing outside it stay owner-required.

<a id="owner-rows"></a>The owner sets policy once and the engine applies it. Every owner-required row is a one-time grant, of one of two shapes:

- **Configuration, set once.** The owner sets it for the project, a rung or the user file, and it holds until the owner changes it: `sandbox`, `sandbox.session_bus`, `env`, `env_file`, `scope`, `ladder.command`, `ladder.web_mcp`, `ladder.reach`, `browser_kit` and `delegation` grant power; `budget.raise` grants money; `merge.admin`, `decision_delegation`, `research.min_sources` and `publish.policy` set how the run goes. None is a step of an ordinary day.
- **One use, for one named case.** The approval binds one task, head, revision, claim or decision and is used up by the change it allowed: `waive.tests`, `waive.clean`, `waive.ci`, `waive.sources`, `waive.review_live`, `task.downgrade`, `task.rerecord_review`, `claim.release`, `decision.delegate` and `task.cancel_needs_owner`. Each loosens a check on work the gates judged, or overrides an owner's or a worker's own hold, so it stays with the owner; an ordinary day reaches none of them. A host-only failure main shares keeps the task submitted and reruns when main moves (`automation.js`), with no waiver; a capped or down reviewer is the operational `waive.review`; a claim whose process exited is released by any agent; the orchestrator's own hold is `task.cancel_hold`.

Two former flow gates are policy-driven: an orchestrator's or the engine's hold is cancelled as `task.cancel_hold`, and `publish` follows the publish policy. Decisions whose tasks closed close themselves ([decisions.json](#decisionsjson)), and a worker's question about a failure main shares goes to the orchestrator.

<a id="publish-policy"></a>`project set --publish-policy '{"conditions": [...]}'` records `publish_policy` in `project.json`; it is `publish.policy`, owner-required, and `null` removes it. The conditions are any of `review_clean` (the release task's latest review gate evidence at its head passed and is not a waiver, which only a reviewer spawned for that task, a clean context, or the owner records), `full_suite` (its latest tests gate evidence at its head passed under current pinned commands and test settings, is not waived, ran tests rather than none mode, did not run mapped suites, and reports passing cases with no skips or unreported cases for every configured host-only file tracked at that head) and `main_ci_green` (a fresh read of the base's runs, the one `inbox` makes and reuses for a minute, finds no red workflow). With a policy set, the orchestrator's `ask --setting publish --change '{"task": "T<n>", ...}'` names the release task; when that task is accepted and every condition holds, it exits 0 with no decision and records a `setting` event on that task with `settings: { publish: "owner-required" }`, `policy: { conditions: [{ name, ok }], sha }` and the `change`, in place of `approved_by`. An owner's approval of the same request is still used first. Otherwise it opens or reuses the owner's decision as without a policy; the decision's `why` and the refusal name each failed condition. An empty `conditions` list publishes any accepted task only after the mandatory main push CI check below passes.

<a id="fallback-route-grants"></a>A personal fallback route can carry authority: a grant (`sandbox`, `env`, `env_file`, `scope`), a harness without an OS sandbox (agy, opencode, pi), the `command` harness, args on an unsandboxed harness, `web_mcp`, `mcp`, or a tool that is not a harness built-in. The user file is a plain file: anything running as the user can edit it, the unsandboxed orchestrator included, and `TOWER_CRANE_CONFIG` can name any file. So such a route counts only when `ladder set RUNG --fallbacks` wrote it under the check above, as the owner or with the owner's approval. That write pins a SHA-256 of each route in `owner/route-grants.json` under the project's recorded `owner_config_dir`, beside the owner key that every agent sandbox hides: `{ "version": 1, "files": { "<user file>": { "<rung>": ["<sha256>" or null, by route position] } } }`. The user file key is its canonical path: the real path, in the case the filesystem stores. So a symlink, another case on a case-insensitive filesystem or a relative `TOWER_CRANE_CONFIG` that reaches the same file finds the same pins, and a link moved to another file after the approval finds that file's pins, not the old ones. A key written by an earlier version under the path as spelled still counts for that spelling while the file has no canonical entry; the next pin write for the file moves every such entry under the canonical key and leaves that key in place, as `{}` when no rung is pinned, so old spellings are never read for that file again. The store records no write times: when two old spellings hold the same rung, the spelling the write came through wins, and a route approved only through the other may ask the owner again. The hash covers the route's `harness`, `command`, `args`, `tools`, `mcp`, `web_mcp` and grants, with keys sorted, plus the content of its `env_file`. So approved grants moved to another route, position or harness, or an `env_file` edited after the approval, are not approved. The harness hashed is the one the route resolves to, so a route that inherits the primary's harness is approved only on that harness: moving the primary, which the orchestrator may do among sandboxed harnesses, asks the owner again. Pins are per position: inserting a route before a pinned one, or reordering the list, asks the owner again for each pinned route that moved. Spawn hands each kept route's approved `env_file` hash to the supervisor, which reads the file again at launch, on each retry and at each switch, and refuses the route with a notice (`skipping fallback N`) when the content no longer matches. The pin holds hashes, never values, which are often secrets. The write takes a lock in that `owner/` directory, since every project bound to it shares the file.

Spawn drops a fallback route that carries authority without a matching pin, and says so in the spawn's `notices`; `ladder show` lists it as a problem with the command that approves it. A route that sets only `model`, `profile`, `provider`, `effort` or `supervision` on a sandboxed harness carries none and needs no pin, so personal fallbacks such as `{"harness":"codex","profile":"astra"}` work as before. A route that inherits its harness carries what it sets; the primary's own move off a sandbox is `ladder.reach`. `ladder set --fallbacks` compares a new list with the pinned routes of the old one, not with the file: a route edited into the file by hand counts as new, so its grants, `command` or `web_mcp` are owner-required and an unconfined harness or non-built-in tool opens `ladder.reach`, even when the write leaves the route as the file holds it.

The pin follows the user file: an approval in one project counts in every project bound to the same `owner_config_dir`. A relative path in a pinned route resolves in each repository that runs it: a `sandbox.write` or `sandbox.read` entry grants a different directory per repository, and a relative `env_file` names a different file, whose content then does not match the pin, so the route is skipped there. Give personal routes absolute or `~/` paths. A project without `owner_config_dir` cannot pin, and `ladder set --fallbacks` refuses such routes there until the owner runs `owner-key`.

The research agent ships with network and web tools enabled in code; initializing or tuning its model does not ask for a new grant. Changing its explicit `web_mcp` command or tools is owner-required, including clearing it to return to native web tools. The orchestrator's attempt opens a decision without changing the rung. A setting that widens network access is owner-required under `ladder.reach`; operational built-in opt-ins must keep the existing sandbox network confinement.

`init` checks the same table; with no state to hold a decision, an orchestrator's owner-required `init` setting is refused and it inits without it. The same holds for what `init` copies from the user file, which anything running as the user can edit and `TOWER_CRANE_CONFIG` can name anywhere. Only the explicit owner's `init` copies the primary rungs whole. Any other `init` copies what `ladder set` lets the orchestrator write and prints a `notice:` line for the rest. It leaves out `sandbox`, `env`, `env_file`, `scope`, `command` and `web_mcp`, tools that are not built-ins keeping the rung's sandbox, and MCP servers the owner's harness config does not define. A rung that would run as a command, or outside its role's sandbox on its own harness or the default one, gets its built-in rung on that rung's built-in harness. A rung that keeps its built-in harness and args is not withheld for them: the built-in rung would run the same way. The `init` event lists what was left out under `withheld`, and so does `--json` output. The owner adds any of it with `ladder set --agent owner`, or approves the decision the orchestrator's `ladder set` opens.

The owner is the resolved name `owner`, supplied explicitly by `--agent owner` or `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT=owner`, from a process that passes the [owner identity rule](#agent-identity): no task variable, no other `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT`, and a terminal or the owner key from the project's recorded `owner_config_dir`; the terminal fallback grants no authority. The orchestrator is an explicit identity that some spawn recorded with role `orchestrator`, or, never spawned, the name `orchestrator` (the owner's interactive session). A name any spawn started as a worker, reviewer or small agent is never the orchestrator, nor is a command the state broker runs, nor a `--agent` that differs from a `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT` naming such an agent. A waived gate counts when the owner recorded it, or the orchestrator for an operational waiver. Any agent may recover a spawned claim when the shared process detector verifies its exit under the state lock. Set `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT=owner` per command, never in a shell profile, where it could override a spawned agent's identity.

## Files

| File | Holds |
|---|---|
| `project.json` | name, goal, repo, base branch, default harness and model ladder, limits, budgets, standards profile, owner policies |
| `tasks.json` | every task and its status, evidence and spend |
| `decisions.json` | questions for the owner and their answers |
| `briefs/<task>.md` | shared task context and optional worker or reviewer sections, kept current by the orchestrator |
| `reviews/<task>-<sha>.md` | the brief's reviewer sections, diff and software gate results prepared by review dispatch; large packets are read from this file to keep argv within Windows limits |
| `events.jsonl` | log of every change, with time and agent; only appended to, except that lock recovery cuts off a torn append of an unfinished commit ([Durability and write order](#durability-and-write-order)) |
| `events.cuts.jsonl` | one line per cut lock recovery made to `events.jsonl`: `{ "at", "id", "size", "from", "ends" }`, the last event id of the commit whose append it cut, the length it cut the log back to, the length before and the offsets where the removed lines ended, plus `"late": true` for a cut an older tool made that a later holder recorded; only appended to, so its length counts the cuts and is the generation byte cursors carry ([Durability and write order](#durability-and-write-order)) |
| `progress/<agent>.jsonl` | the agent's tool activity, `hook progress` rows appended without the state lock; the supervisor watches the file for activity, and no state decision reads it |
| `evidence/<task>/<sha256>.json` | one settled evidence field over 1 KiB, as compact JSON named by its SHA-256; never changes once written ([tasks.json](#tasksjson)) |
| `sketch.md`, `sketch.html` | rendered views; regenerated by `tower-crane render` and after every write |
| `sketch.stamp` | `{ "cuts": BYTES, "log": BYTES, "at": MS }`, the length of `events.cuts.jsonl`, the event log size and the time of the state the published sketch shows, compared in that order, so a slower render of older state does not replace it; a stamp without `cuts` counts as 0 |
| `lock` | the write lock, a directory; never edit |
| `dispatch/lock` | the worker dispatch lock, a directory taken the same way as `lock`, held by a worker `spawn` from its readiness check until its spawn is recorded or refused, while it may run git ([Dispatch lock](#dispatch-lock)); never edit |
| `logs/<task>-<agent>.log` | output of agents started by `tower-crane spawn` |
| `logs/<task>-<agent>-startup-failed-<time>.log` | the output of a supervisor that did not report its start, kept when it wrote any or when the dispatch's startup wait ran out. Its last line is `tower-crane: startup phases in ms: ` and the JSON of the spawn event's `startup_ms`, with the phases that finished; the refusal prints its path and last lines. The newest 5 per task, by modification time, are kept; each new or moved-aside one counts as the newest and removes the older ones |
| `logs/ci-<task>-<sha>-<unique>/output.log` | one local CI run's stdout and stderr joined by whole lines, escapes removed and redacted; the newest 5 runs per task are kept ([Local CI output](#local-ci-output)) |
| `ci-artifacts/<run>/` | the files a failed local CI run named, copied out of the gate worktree before removal, text redacted; hidden from every agent sandbox; `ci-artifacts/.gitignore` ignores everything ([Local CI output](#local-ci-output)) |
| `homes/<agent>/` | the home `tower-crane spawn` builds fresh for each claude, codex and agy agent: its harness config directory with generated settings, instructions and permission profile, `git` and `gh` shims, links to the user's credentials, for a sandboxed claude agent `tmp`, its `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` ([agent files and homes](ladder.md#agent-files-and-homes)), and `home/`, the agent's `HOME`, which for a sandboxed codex agent also holds the shims it runs, `home/.tower-crane-shims/` (`bin/`, `policy.json` and a copy of `lib/`), since its sandbox hides the rest of `homes/<agent>/`; plus what the harness writes there (transcripts). Private to the user; `homes/.gitignore` ignores everything. A spawn removes a home only after the agent and every live controller using its tool snapshot have exited. `homes/.codex/<agent>/sessions/` keeps that agent's codex sessions for usage after its home is gone ([Agent files and homes](ladder.md#agent-files-and-homes)) |
| `brokers/<agent>/` | `broker.json`, the [state broker](ladder.md#state-broker)'s address and token for a sandboxed agent (and its `broker.sock` for claude), while the agent runs; only that agent's sandbox can read it. The broker writes `brokers/<agent>/.gitignore` when it creates that directory, never in an existing directory or its parent. Closing removes a directory it created; an existing directory keeps its other files |
| `push/` | the [state broker](ladder.md#state-broker)'s repository for the pushes sandboxed agents ask for: `cache.git`, which holds only objects git hashed on arrival and stays between pushes, and the `cache.lock` directory while a push uses it; no sandbox can write here. `push/.gitignore` ignores everything |
| `skills/<key>/` | the role skills every spawned agent is told to read: a copy of `skills/` from `TOWER_CRANE_PLUGIN_ROOT` or the dispatching package, named by the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of its file names and contents. Spawn copies the skills to a temporary directory beside it and renames that to the key, retrying a rename the system refuses for a moment (EPERM, EACCES or EBUSY, as Windows does while a scanner holds new files) for up to 2 s; a key another dispatch wrote first is kept, and the retry stops once it is there. Spawn writes a key once and never removes it, so a path an agent was given stays valid after any agent exits. Prompts, home instructions, the codex, opencode and agy skill links, pi's `--skill` and `reviews/<task>-<sha>.md` name it. It is outside `homes/`, so the claude and codex sandboxes, which open the state directory read-only, can read it |
| `gitconfig/<agent>` | the global git config of a sandboxed claude or codex agent and its harness (`GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL`), rendered from the user's global git config ([Shared git directory](ladder.md#shared-git-directory)); their sandboxes read it and never write it. A spawn removes it with the agent's home. `gitconfig/.gitignore` ignores everything |

Each spawn pins its tool runtime in `homes/<agent>/tool/`. `homes/<agent>/tool.json` and the `spawn` event's `detail.tool` record `{sha, version, path}`. `sha` is the tool checkout's HEAD, or null for an installed package without Git metadata; `version` is the package version. The snapshot copies `bin/`, `lib/`, `agents/`, `skills/`, `standards/` and `package.json`, without links back to the checkout. Skill paths the agent is given name the skill store (`skills/<key>/`), not this snapshot, which its sandbox cannot read. The generated Git and gh shims, harness hooks, notify command and supervisor resolve this snapshot. Its policy is generated by the same tool version. Supervisors, containment keepers and host CLI controllers hold the snapshot they execute, even when it belongs to another agent. The engine writes PID identity holds under `tool/.users/`; launchers register child holds before releasing their own, and pruning removes dead holds only after verifying exit. An unobservable user keeps its hold. A home rebuild preserves a held `tool/` and its holds. After all users exit, a later spawn prunes it with the home, and a new dispatch gets the tool version then installed.

Pinning keeps upgrades independent of live dispatches. On 2026-10-08, moving the orchestrator checkout to T112 (`33d7eb5`) made existing shims read new requirements against policies without `branch` or `repo`. Pushes from T30 and T79 had to be completed by hand. Copying the runtime at dispatch prevents a checkout move from changing a live agent's policy reader or hook protocol. Upgrades take effect on the next dispatch, with no feature flag.

The shim defaults missing `gitPush` to `none`, `gh` to `[]` and `hook` to null. Missing branch and repository default to null and deny remote pushes. Before checking permissions, a pre-T112 branch policy with absent `branch` or `repo` migrates those fields in memory from its recorded task and project; a field state does not record migrates to null, which refuses remote pushes (no branch or no repository to bind them to) and leaves every other command allowed. Migration requires its protected `hook.json` in the same home and matching dispatch agent, task and state environment; it never infers authority from the current Git branch or remote. Missing recorded values or a mismatched binding refuse the call. The policy file stays read-only, and all calls use the same migrated shape and push checks. The historical policy fixture is generated from `lib/agents.js` and the worker frontmatter at `e034a4325677ada9d0752a1acee60483acfd8849`, T112's parent.

Codex homes rebuild `config.toml` and every `<profile>.config.toml` with explicit allowlists for provider, MCP server and per-tool fields. Provider sub-tables keep only named fields: `aws.region` and `aws.profile`, and `query_params.api-version`; a credential-shaped name (containing key, bearer, token, secret or password) never crosses from a sub-table, and `aws.credential_export` and `aws.auth_refresh` stay outside. Unknown fields, credential fields, literal env and headers, and fields with unexpected types are omitted. Named environment-variable references remain for explicit MCP opt-ins; the browser kit also removes `env_vars` and `env_http_headers`. Auth files remain linked to the user's originals.

Codex model and auth-store fields also require their expected scalar types in base configs, profile files and legacy `[profiles.NAME]` tables; arrays and tables are omitted. Claude's `mcp.json` keeps only string `type`, `command` and `url` fields and `args` arrays containing only strings. A nested credential table cannot cross through these settings.

A brief without role headings is shared by worker dispatch and role-filtered brief readers. A brief can add level-two headings `## Shared`, `## Worker` and `## Reviewer`; matching is case-insensitive and headings inside fenced code blocks are ignored. Text before the first role heading is shared; each section ends at the next role heading. The `## Rework notes` heading is also a shared section boundary, so corrections appended by `rework` stay visible to each role. Worker dispatch and `brief get` send shared text followed by the matching role section, and omit the other role's section. Review dispatch sends only reviewer sections alongside the diff, acceptance and gate results. `brief get` infers the role from a `worker-` or `reviewer-` agent name, or accepts an explicit `--role`; owner and orchestrator identities see the full brief by default. `brief set` warns on stderr when it finds a reviewer section without a worker section.

All JSON files carry `"version": 1`. Writes go to a temp file in the same directory, synced, and are renamed into place; the directory is then synced so the rename survives a power loss. Windows cannot open a directory to sync it, so there the rename is as durable as the file system makes it; file systems that refuse a directory sync are treated the same way. Windows refuses to open or stat a state file while another process renames a new copy over it, with EPERM, EACCES or EBUSY, until that process closes it. A writer retries such a refused rename, and a reader retries such a refused read, for up to 2 s before it fails. A lock-free read can therefore wait up to 2 s per file on Windows; the supervisor's periodic read is one, so a concurrent commit does not stop its worker. On other platforms these codes are a permission failure, and a read fails on the first one. A refused state mutation writes nothing. `accept` can record software gate results before refusing acceptance.

### Durability and write order

A command returns only after its writes are on disk. The event append to `events.jsonl` is synced before the command reports success, so an acknowledged `msg`, hook delivery or mutation survives a power loss.

A commit that changes `project.json`, `tasks.json` or `decisions.json` and logs events writes in this order:

1. `commit.<id>.json` is written in the state directory, where `<id>` is the id of the commit's last event. It records the device, inode and byte length of `events.jsonl` before the append and the ids of all the commit's events. The inode is in `ino` as a JSON number, as released tools write and read it, and in `ino_exact` as a decimal string. A number rounds Windows file ids past 2^53, so recovery compares `ino_exact` when the manifest has it and `ino` otherwise. Each changed file is written in full and synced as `<name>.json.<id>.commit`. The directory is synced.
2. The events are appended to `events.jsonl` and the log is synced. This is the commit point.
3. Each staged file is renamed over its target, the directory is synced and `commit.<id>.json` is removed.

A commit with events and no file change does step 2 only. A commit that changes files and logs no event writes each file with a synced temp file and a rename, then syncs the directory.

The next process to take the state lock, before it reads anything, finishes a commit whose holder died. A staged file whose `<id>` is the id on the last complete line of `events.jsonl` reached its commit point: the log is synced, then the file is renamed into place. Any other staged file is deleted; its last event was never logged and the command never returned. A power loss can tear the append and keep the commit's first lines, or a last line missing only its newline, which the next append would complete. Recovery cuts those bytes off, truncating the log back to the recorded length, only when `events.jsonl` is still the same file (device and inode) and every line after that length, complete or torn, is one of the commit's own events. Before the cut it writes the cut to `cut.<id>.json`; after the cut is synced, and before the holder appends anything, it appends `{ "at", "id", "size", "from", "ends" }` to `events.cuts.jsonl`, starting with a newline when a torn record ends the file, and syncs it. It removes `cut.<id>.json` after the staged files and the manifest. A holder that dies between the cut and the record leaves `cut.<id>.json` beside the manifest, and the next holder, finding the log at the cut's length, appends the record unless one with the same `id` is already there, so two cuts at the same place and of the same length are both recorded. Tools through 0.1.2 finish such a recovery without recording the cut: they remove the staged files and the manifest and leave `cut.<id>.json`. The next holder that finds `cut.<id>.json` alone appends the record with `"late": true`, since the older tool may have appended past the cut meanwhile. Anything else after it, such as an event an older tool appended or a log restored from a copy, stays, and the staged files are only deleted. A command whose append fails keeps its staged files when its last event reached the log anyway (a failed sync after the write), so the next holder renames them into place; otherwise it applies the same rule. A staged file that cannot be removed (Windows keeps a file another process holds open) stops the holder with an error before it appends anything, so no later write is judged against an old commit. Only a dead or failed holder leaves staged files, and nothing is appended to the log until the next holder has recovered, so a crash never leaves a state change without its events. Between steps 2 and 3, and after a crash until the next lock holder, the log holds events the files do not show yet. A reader without the lock reads the log, then the files, and keeps the result only if the log did not change meanwhile: same size, device and inode, and the bytes before that size still end with the last line it read. The inode is compared exactly: Windows file ids run past 2^53, where a Number would round neighbouring files to one id. The last line catches recovery cutting a torn tail off the same file and the next commit appending as many bytes, which keeps the size and the inode. The reader skips reading that line when the log's modification and change times are the ones it read and its last change was at least one timestamp step (20 ms, or 2 s for a time in whole seconds) before the read began, since any later change gets a later time; an idle log then costs no read. That needs file times from this machine's clock that move on every write, so it holds only on local disks. On Linux those are the local filesystem types (ext2/3/4, xfs, btrfs, tmpfs, ramfs, f2fs, zfs, bcachefs, overlayfs); on NFS, SMB, 9p, virtiofs or FUSE the reader always compares the line. macOS numbers filesystem types as it registers them (statfs `f_type`; Node does not report the name, `f_fstypename`), so there it holds when the state directory's filesystem has the same type as the root, which is APFS on every macOS Node runs on; SMB, NFS, AFP, WebDAV, FUSE and HFS+ mounts compare. On Windows a state directory whose real path is a UNC share (`\\server\share`, which a mapped network drive also resolves to) compares, and one on a drive letter does not. Other platforms, and a directory whose type cannot be read, always compare. A process that keeps one event reader compares on every read of it, so no read vouches for bytes it did not check. A commit appends before it renames, so then no rename landed during the read but those of the commit the log ends with. When the last event it read is the last complete line of the log and staged files carry that id, the commit reached its commit point, and the reader reads those staged files in place of the files they replace; a staged file renamed meanwhile is read from its target, which holds the same text. Staged files of any other id are never used: their commit point was not reached, or other lines follow it in the log, and recovery deletes them. A reader can open and drop the staged files of a torn last line it parsed. A reader whose log changed reads again. One that loses five times in a row waits up to 500 ms for the lock and reads once under it, so a lock-free reader can briefly wait. If it cannot take the lock in that time, or cannot write the state directory, it returns files with events read after them, which are never older than the files, as every read was before. So a reader that has an event also sees the change it records: `wait`, and a command run after `wait` returns, read the state the woken event's commit left. `validate` still takes the lock, to recover an interrupted commit before it reports. Every release from 0.1.0 on stages, logs and recovers commits this way, so released versions can share a state directory: whichever takes the lock after a crash finishes or discards the commit by the same rules, and `validate` recovers under the lock before it reports, so it reports no drift for a commit another version left half done. Development builds from before staged commits do not recover them; a commit they make after a crash leaves the staged files unmatched, and the next recovering holder deletes them and leaves the log alone. Recovery checks `project.json.schema_version` before it touches staged files: under a newer schema it refuses, as a read does, and leaves them for the newer tool ([Schema compatibility](#schema-compatibility)). A change to how commits are staged or recovered raises the schema and gives the staged files new names, so released tools that do not check the schema first never match them. `cut.<id>.json` is the exception: it adds a file beside the staged ones and changes neither their names nor what an older tool does with them. A tool through 0.1.2 that finishes such a recovery cuts the same bytes the newer one would, by the same rule, or finds them already cut; it only leaves the cut unrecorded and the file in place. The next newer holder records it as `late` from that file, and readers treat a late record by what they read, so the older tool's events appended after the cut are neither skipped nor read twice.

Windows keeps a file while another process (a scanner, a backup tool) holds it open without delete sharing. When that stops recovery for longer than 2 s, renaming a staged file into place or removing one, the holder releases the lock and the attempt counts as a busy lock: a writer retries until its deadline and then exits 3 with `state is busy: another process holds an interrupted commit's file open`, and a try-lock (the orchestrator lease renewal of a read command) reports exit 3 at once and leaves the renewal to a later command.

A writer killed between writing a temp file (`<name>.<pid>.<random>.tmp`, used for writes that log no event) and renaming it leaves the file behind. Each lock holder deletes such files in the state directory whose pid is not alive and that are older than 60 s; a recent one may belong to a writer in another pid namespace.

On macOS, Node's file sync is `fcntl(F_FULLFSYNC)`, which flushes the drive's write cache; libuv falls back to `F_BARRIERFSYNC` and then plain `fsync` where a filesystem refuses it. Linux `fsync` flushes the cache already. Windows syncs a file with `FlushFileBuffers` and cannot sync a directory, so a rename there is as durable as NTFS makes it.

A reader that tails the log without the lock (`wait`, `serve`, the `wait` host's state history, a gate run it joined) checks the length of `events.cuts.jsonl` on each read that finds the log's times changed. When it grew and a new record's `size` is below where the reader stopped, the reader goes back to that `size` and reads on from there, so it neither resumes in the middle of a line nor skips the events appended after the cut, even when the log has grown past where it stopped. The events it had read past that point were never committed: `wait` has delivered them, `serve` drops them from its message history, and the state history is read again. `wait`, `GET /api/messages`, the `mcp__tower-crane__watch` follower and the orchestrator's Stop hold hand out a byte cursor with the length of `events.cuts.jsonl` its reader had applied (`OFFSET:CUTS` for `wait --after`, `after` and `cuts` for the API). Given back, it is read from the `size` of every cut recorded after that length whose range holds the offset, and from the offset otherwise, so it resolves exactly. A plain byte cursor (`wait --after OFFSET`, `GET /api/messages?after=OFFSET`) does not say when it was taken. It is read from a recorded cut's `size` when it ends one of the lines the cut removed and does not end a line of the log as it is now. Every cursor handed out after a cut ends a line of the log as it is then, so it never moves and a wait re-armed with its own cursor does not see its event again. A plain cursor from before the cut whose offset a new line happens to end at too stays where it is, and its reader misses the events between the cut and that offset. A late record moves any cursor only by that plain rule, and moves a tailing reader back only when the bytes it last read are no longer the log's. `wait` carries the length of `events.cuts.jsonl` from the moment it took its cursor to its reader, so a cut recorded in between also moves it. A reader checks the length again after each read of the log. A cut recorded during the read joins the cursor it hands out when its `size` is at or past the offset the read reached, or when the read started at or before its `size` and the last line and fragment the read took are still the log's.
