Both flags appear in the evidence and its audit event. A cleanup scan with checks that did not run records `infrastructure_failure: true`.

Tests and clean evidence and their audit events carry `gate_policy`: `{tests_cmd, tests_proof_cmd, paths, keep, expensive}` for tests or `{clean_cmd}` for clean, captured when the check starts. The tests settings are the effective values: `paths` is `tests.paths` (null for the default layouts), `keep` is `tests.keep` (null when unset, which keeps the default manifests and lockfiles) and `expensive` is `tests.expensive === true`. While a map is pinned outside crucial mode, the tests policy also snapshots `tests_map`, `ci_required` and `ci_local`; with `tests.crucial` set it records `crucial`. When configured, it also records `host_only` and a `host_env_sha256` digest of the sorted host environment map, so changes invalidate old proof without duplicating environment values in receipts. Command values are trimmed strings or null. Missing, malformed or changed policy makes previous evidence stale, even if the owner changes commands during a run. Successful receipts must also include the pinned test command or mapped head expansion (except none mode) or the pinned cleanup invocation with its appended arguments. Scoped proof records `receipt.proof_tests`, the changed test paths used to expand the pinned template; mapped head runs also record `receipt.head_tests`; every test shell receipt must match the full command, the mapped head expansion, the proof expansion or a host-only expansion of the pinned proof template for one file in `receipt.host_only.results`. Host-only expansions count even when all cases in the recorded file skipped. The proof command from the run without the change still counts; any other extra shell command is rejected. Map, expensive-policy and required-job changes invalidate mapped-policy receipts. Acceptance, merge and task reports use the same check. Old receipts without this snapshot require a new check; owner waivers retain their existing behavior.

The CLI refuses manual software verdicts, and software receipts require matching records in tasks.json and events.jsonl. Plain files cannot stop a writer running as the same user from forging those records or an owner waiver. Command receipts show what ran; acceptance checks them and their recorded policy against the pinned commands. The reviewer checks that the configured runners verify the change.

Research Claude fallback routes in the user file inherit the primary `web_mcp` unless they specify their own. The same `{name, command, args}` validation applies to an explicit fallback server. Other harnesses do not inherit it. A route combining an inherited or explicit web MCP with `mcp` is reported and skipped so only the web server loads.

### Research sources

`project.research` is optional and accepts only `min_sources`, a positive integer; it defaults to 10. The explicit owner sets it with `project set --research-min-sources N` (also accepted by `init`). Changing the minimum invalidates sources evidence recorded under a different minimum.

Tasks of kind `research` require sources evidence on every tier. Kind `research` selects the research tier by default. Changing only the kind preserves the existing tier and its role; verification follows the new kind. Tier changes do not remove the sources requirement or citation-review instructions. Code tasks may use the research tier for model selection and retain their code gates.

A research deliverable is committed at `research/<task id>.json`:

```json
{
  "sources": [{"id": "s1", "url": "https://example.org/page"}],
  "claims": [{"claim": "The supported conclusion", "quote": "Exact text from the page", "source": "s1"}]
}
```

The example shows one citation; the default requires at least ten. Every source has a unique non-blank id and an HTTP(S) URL without credentials. Every claim has non-blank `claim`, `quote` and `source` strings; the source names an entry, and every source must be cited. Multiple claims may cite one source. `check sources ID` reads this file through Git at the submitted sha, fetches each page at check time, and finds every quote in its page text. It normalizes Unicode and whitespace, removes inline tags without adding spaces, separates block boundaries, strips comments and script/style/template content, and decodes common and numeric HTML entities. A single-pass scanner tracks quoted tag attributes, nested comments and templates, and raw script/style blocks. Unfinished blocks stay hidden; an unfinished tag or an unquoted `<` inside a tag excludes the rest of the page from matching. Punctuation adjacent to inline markup stays adjacent. It accepts HTML and plain text; PDF, JavaScript-rendered, authenticated and other non-text pages need a publicly fetchable text alternative. Quote matching proves presence; independent review checks source credibility and whether each quote supports its claim, including uncited factual claims elsewhere in the deliverable.

Before each connection, the gate resolves the hostname and refuses any non-public IPv4 or IPv6 answer, including loopback, private, link-local, mapped and special-use addresses. It pins the full validated address list for the socket while retaining the hostname for HTTP Host, TLS SNI and certificate validation. Node can fall back between IPv6 and IPv4 without resolving again; every attempt shares the page timeout. Redirects are followed manually, with the same URL and DNS checks at every hop, up to five redirects. DNS resolution and all redirect hops share the page timeout. The CLI has no setting that permits private addresses; local tests inject a resolver and transport into the gate context.

The gate rejects duplicate URLs (fragments do not distinguish pages), duplicate final redirect URLs, pages with the same extracted text, dead links, missing quotes and uncited sources. Each fetch is limited to ten seconds and 5 MiB; the gate has a two minute deadline to bound stalled responses. Sources evidence carries a `receipt` with `min_sources`, `deliverable` and the fetched `sources`: id, requested and final URL, HTTP status, text SHA-256, fetch timestamp and cited claims. The audit event carries the same receipt; it also records the Git commands resolving and reading the submitted commit. The reviewer context includes this receipt and the instruction to map each claim to its source. A new check always fetches again; acceptance reuses evidence at the same sha, revision and minimum.

### Ready and blocked

A task is ready when its status is `todo` or `rework`, every dependency is `accepted`, `needs_owner` is null, and no open decision lists it in `blocks`. Otherwise `tower-crane ready --all` says what blocks it.

Ready tasks come in priority order: urgent first, then most outstanding dependents (direct or transitive, not yet accepted or cancelled), then by id.

### Release for a decision

`release ID --reason R --pr N` gives back a claim whose work waits on a decision and links PR `N` to the task. `N` must be open and is checked as `submit` checks it. Its head branch must equal the task's `branch`, except when the task has none or moves from a closed or merged PR to `N`: then it becomes the task's `branch`, and the task's `sha` clears, since that submission belongs to the PR it replaces. A task linked to another open PR keeps that PR and refuses the release; once that PR is closed or merged, `N` replaces it. The `pr` and `branch` values stay when the claim is released. The link is written after the owner check, so a release that opens an owner decision and refuses writes no link. The release note records `linked PR #N on branch B`.

The release event's `detail.decisions` lists the ids of the open decisions that blocked the task when it was released. A todo task whose latest release has them can be sent back with `rework`, answered or not: its status becomes `rework` and `revision` advances as for any rework. A todo task released without decisions still refuses `rework`.

Answering the decision removes its block, so the task is ready as `todo` with its PR linked. Its next dispatch continues that PR: when the task's branch is not local, the worktree starts from the branch head on origin, even before any submission, and the worker prompt names the PR. A branch that origin no longer has starts from the base a new task would use.

### Spawned process exits

On Windows, an npm `.cmd` harness runs as a direct Node process with the shim's script and the original arguments. Spawn events retain the configured harness and route; the recorded pid belongs to the Node process. The supervisor uses the same resolution for retries and fallback routes.

`status` and `ready`, including `ready --all`, report a current claim when its matching spawned process has exited without submitting. This is a computed diagnostic, not a stored status. Their JSON includes `exited_claims: [{ id, agent, pid, log, code, size, tail }]`. `code` is the recorded foreground exit code or null when unknown; `size` is the log's size in bytes or null when unavailable. Text names the process and log, includes a tail of at most 20 lines from the final 8192 bytes, and directs any agent to recover the verified exit with `release --reason`. It never prints a command that supplies owner identity. Missing logs leave the exit visible with a `log unavailable` diagnostic. Foreground spawns retain logs; legacy events with `log: null` have an empty tail.

A `spawn phase` event with `active: false` for that spawn's agent, attempt and pid proves the exit from the event log, so every observer gets the same answer; the supervisor records it only after the harness exited, its process group is gone (on Windows, where the monitor does not track the harness's descendants after it exits, only the harness pid) and no retry or fallback route remains. Without such a record or a `spawn exit` receipt, the views probe the pid. Only the current claimant's most recent spawn counts. A worker can claim after its spawn; earlier claims, release, submit and rework events fence off older spawn records. Reclaiming an expired lease under the same agent identity does not inherit the previous claim's spawn. A submitted task or a manual claim with no matching spawn is not an exited claim. Reading either view writes no state, changes no lease and starts no replacement.

The host recorded by `spawn` bounds local pid checks to that host; older events without it are assumed local. Permission errors and processes on another host do not establish an exit. Linux checks recognize zombie processes and compare the recorded process start time to catch a reused pid. Other platforms use missing pids and recorded foreground exits.

A worker spawn event records the task's `revision`. The supervisor's write of a worker's `spawn exit` also applies the respawn cap: when the task has 3 worker exits at its current revision without a submit, counted since its last `submit`, `owner-done` or `task update` that cleared `needs_owner`, and the task has no owner ask yet, it sets `needs_owner` and appends `respawn cap` with `{ cap, exits, revision, agents, log, needs_owner }`. A run whose monitor died has no `spawn exit`; its `worker-exited` counts instead, and the observer's write that records it applies the cap. Exits with `availability_failure: true`, runs ended by an `interrupt` or a `budget stop`, and runs whose own worker's `release` (its `holder`) records open `decisions` do not count. A `worker-exited` does not count while that run's monitor is still running; its `spawn exit` decides. `task show` adds `respawn_cap: { exits, cap, agents, log, tail }` while that ask holds, with the tail read from the log at show time; state never stores it.

Any agent recovers a verified exited task with `release ID --reason R`. The detector rechecks under the lock; a live or unverified process requires the claimant or the owner; the orchestrator's attempt opens a decision. The usual prior `todo` or `rework` status is restored. The supplied reason stays in the release note; only the observed pid, log path, exit code and log size are preserved in an additional note and the release event's `detail.exited_spawn`. Release does not read or persist log text; the views read the tail live from the log file. Releasing clears the diagnostic; a new claim does not inherit the old process.

### Acceptance gates

Failed GitHub commands retain the child exit status, signal and startup error code and message in the gate evidence `summary`, including when stderr and stdout are empty.

`tower-crane accept` refuses unless the task's current revision has, at the submitted `sha`:

- `code` tasks: `tests` ok, `clean` ok, and `review` ok from an independent reviewer
- `research` tasks on any tier: `sources` ok and `review` ok from an independent reviewer
- other tasks: `review` ok from an independent reviewer
- any task with a PR, whatever its kind: `ci` ok as well

An independent reviewer is the owner, or a reviewer that `spawn` dispatched for that task at that sha and revision, never the submitter. Review evidence that does not count also does not hold back the review dispatch `accept` makes, does not raise the review rung and is not passed to a rework worker as a finding. The board keeps it in the ledger with a does-not-count reason; neither its pass nor its fail verdict sets the review gate indicator.

Tests modes change how `check tests` produces evidence, not which gates acceptance requires. A code task in mode `none` still needs audited tests evidence for its submitted sha, plus cleanup and independent review.

Successful tests evidence counts only when its audited `tests_mode` matches the mode currently resolved for the project and task kind. A mode change, a missing mode on older evidence or a malformed current policy needs a new tests check. The audit event must contain the same mode as the evidence; changing the mode in tasks.json alone cannot retarget evidence. Owner waivers still apply. Task views, board gate pips and evidence ledger (including accepted tasks), acceptance and merge use this same rule.

Before acceptance, `accept ID` runs missing or stale software gates in order: tests and clean for code, sources for research kind on every tier, then CI for every task with a PR. Tests and clean use the pinned commands. Without a test pin, missing tests in prove or run-only mode block acceptance. None mode records its audited tests result without a command. Optional `--cmd` and `--proof-cmd` must match the pins. Expensive prove uses the pinned template with the `{tests}` placeholder. Eligible current successful receipts are reused. Stale successful receipts, including local CI for an older submitted head or merged tree, run again through the same gate runner under the host governor. Current failures or unaudited attempted receipts require an explicit `check` rerun. The progress message names the software gate being run and lists review separately. Results are recorded even if a later gate refuses acceptance. A head, revision or status change during a gate refuses the attempt. Review dispatch is guarded again under the state lock.

A gate that runs a command on this host (`tests`, `clean`, and `ci` with `ci.local` outside a hotfix) runs once per task, head and revision at a time. Before its command starts, it records a `gate run` event with `{type, sha, revision, source, phase: "running", deadline, pid, host, start_ticks}`, where `source` is `gate:<uuid>` and `deadline` is the start plus, for every timed command the run may start, its full timeout and one minute for the metadata queries around it (one minute when it plans none). A tests run in `prove` mode plans the head run, the scoped proof when `tests.expensive` is true, the reverted run and a wait budget plus a command budget per test file tracked at the head; each of the first three adds one command per `tests.host_only` file tracked at the head. Base-file planning uses `tests.paths` or the default layouts, excludes support files and falls back to the checkout when git cannot list the head. It budgets at least one base file for the full-command fallback. The lease conservatively budgets a command timeout plus a metadata minute for joining a cache holder and the same allowance for worktree setup and a fresh run, for each counted base file. The actual selected commands share one base-step timeout budget. When `git ls-tree` fails twice, the count falls back to the matching files in the checkout on disk under each glob's leading directories, skipping `.git` and `node_modules`. A `run-only` tests run plans the head run, its host-only commands and the same base-file budget; `none` plans no test command. Each declared host probe adds one command at `gates.host_probe_timeout_min`, plus a metadata minute, in every mode. Head, proof and reverted commands each get `gates.tests_timeout_min`; selected base commands receive the remaining base-step budget. Clean plans one command at `gates.clean_timeout_min`, and local CI one at its resolved `timeout`. The same write that records its evidence records `phase: "done"` with `evidence_at`, the evidence's `at`, and `interrupted: true` when `wait` shutdown interrupted the run. A run that throws first records `phase: "error"` with `error`. When the orchestrator guard refuses that write because the orchestrator lease changed, the process records it as an engine write, or else with its next lease. `accept`, `check`, `gates retry` and automation reactions that reach the same gate while such a run is live join it instead of starting a second copy: they print `[TYPE] joining the TYPE run ...` naming its agent, pid, host and start, and, for a holder that cannot be observed, its deadline and the minutes left to it, follow the event log's tail for the lease's end, read its evidence and continue as if they had run it. `check --json` then carries the joined run as `joined: {source, agent, via, pid, host, at}`. A holder whose pid and Linux start ticks show it alive is waited for however long it runs, since a prove-mode tests gate runs several timed commands. A holder that cannot be observed (another host, macOS or Windows where no start ticks are recorded, no permission to signal it) is waited for until its deadline, so a healthy run of any length within its plan is joined, not run twice; past it, the caller prints that it treats the run as ended, runs the gate and records `replaces` with the old lease's source. A holder whose process has exited, a run that ended without evidence, and an interrupted run leave the gate to the caller. Hosted CI and `sources` read remote state and take no lease. `gate run` events are quiet bookkeeping: they wake no `wait` and do not count as stack state changes.

When software gates pass and no review has been attempted at the current head and revision, `accept` dispatches a reviewer and exits 0 with `review_pending: true` and its agent name in JSON; the task stays submitted. A repeated accept reuses a live review dispatch. Call accept again after review evidence arrives. A current independent FAIL makes `accept` and a real `spawn --role review` run `rework --from-review` and exit 1 before software gates or another review dispatch, except the PR-body correction path below. If the linked findings cannot be fetched or matched, they refuse and keep the submission unchanged. `spawn --role review` otherwise refuses until all software gates pass; `--dry-run` checks them for a submitted task without reworking it. For an unsubmitted task a dry run previews the fallback command only, and cannot dispatch a review.

Failed review evidence may include `findings`, a non-empty array listing every blocker as `{ "kind": "...", "path": "...", "claim": "..." }`, with non-blank strings and no other fields. Only when every finding has `kind: "false-claim"` and `path: "PR body"`, `path: "CHANGELOG.md"` or a single Markdown fragment under `changelog.d/` does it qualify for a light check. The reviewer uses this kind only when a prose correction resolves the finding without changing behavior or a contract. Missing metadata, other kinds, other locations and mixed blockers keep the full review path. In particular, `docs/cli.md` and `docs/state.md` claims remain contract blockers.

When every finding is in the PR body, automation dispatches a worker restricted to editing that body. The task stays submitted at the same revision and keeps its SHA and software evidence; false claims still block until fresh independent review evidence passes. `rework --from-review` refuses with correction instructions, without changing state. Submissions without a linked PR and recorded branch retain the explicit body edit and `accept` path.

PR-body repair uses the task worker rung and the supported worker session resume path. Its `spawn` event carries `body_fix` with the failed review snapshot, submitted head and revision, PR, branch, original body hash, `title` and `base`. It takes a worker reservation, not a claim, so the task stays submitted. Status shows `fixing PR body` while its repair or light check runs. The repair prompt replaces the ordinary worker workflow and carries its repair instructions once. The broker refuses commits pushed through it, claim and submit calls, and PR writes other than body edits. On exit, automation reads the current body, PR head, title and base and checks the local head and tracked checkout files before calling the light `accept` path. Untracked files, including a body file written for `gh pr edit --body-file`, do not block the repair. The base may match either the dispatch snapshot or the current expected target from `stack.targetBase`, allowing the engine or GitHub to retarget a dependent PR after its parent merges. This comparison uses state read after the GitHub request, so a concurrent parent merge counts. A moved head, changed tracked file, title or unexpected base enters normal rework, including for an unsandboxed harness.

Light checks use the ordinary reviewer exit policy. A retryable crash or outage, or a missing receipt after the monitor window expires, requests a replacement check at the same head without another body edit. A monitor that can still record its exit remains pending. A clean exit without a verdict or the three-crash reviewer cap holds dispatch and exposes `review_held`, even while the original body-only failure is the latest review evidence. Status stops showing `fixing PR body` for an exited, held reviewer. The item's `accept ID` action retries the corrected body's check after the cause is handled. Exits without a verdict do not count toward the two body-check failure limit.

The light reviewer spawn carries `body_recheck`, and its evidence records `body_recheck: true`. Two consecutive failed automatic body-only checks at the current head and revision stop further automatic repairs, leaving `review_body_stuck` in inbox with the latest summary, structured blockers and a `correction` hint to edit the body before running its `accept ID` action. The initial full review failure does not count, so two repair rounds fit. A fixer that makes no body edit also stops and supplies its reason, except an exit marked `availability_failure` gets one further repair dispatch with `body_fix.availability_retry: true`. Another exit without an edit stops; an outage after a successful edit still allows the light check. The `review body stuck` event preserves that stop across reaction replays and restarts. A passing verdict clears the stop for that submission. The orchestrator can correct the body and request another check with explicit `accept`; it can use ordinary `rework --reason` for code changes.

A prose-only failure that names `CHANGELOG.md` or a `changelog.d/` fragment needs a new head, so it goes through `rework --from-review` like any failure; the `rework` event records `from_review: true`. When the next revision is submitted with no eligible review yet, its review uses the latest eligible failed review of the previous revision, provided that `from_review` rework moved the task from it at the reviewed SHA. The light path then requires that the diff since that review changes only its named changelog fragments. A submitter may also resubmit a changelog-only head at the same revision before rework. The new head needs software gates as usual. Other changes, an unreadable correction diff, any other revision change, ineligible evidence and a later passing review restore full review. Prose-only failures do not escalate worker or reviewer rungs. Introduced security or data-loss defects still block.

Unfinished CI blocks dispatch without requiring rework. The orchestrator watches it through a gate runner, then re-runs `check ci` when it completes before accepting again. Reviewers judge code and acceptance; missing or unfinished CI evidence is reported to the orchestrator rather than recorded as a failed code review. Review policy and prices are operational: the orchestrator or the owner changes them, and a worker asks the orchestrator.

The reviewer receives the submitted diff, task acceptance, audited software gate results (including command receipts, sha, revision and tests mode) and only the brief's `## Reviewer` section. Claude and Codex put the role instructions, rule contents, standards and the repository's review contract sections in the system context; task JSON, brief, gates and diff stay in the user message. Claude writes it to `system.md` in its home and uses `--append-system-prompt-file` and `--exclude-dynamic-system-prompt-sections`; Codex uses its generated `AGENTS.md`. Repository rule labels are relative to the checkout, while external rule paths stay absolute, so moving to another worktree does not change unchanged contents. Every Claude spawn sets `FORCE_PROMPT_CACHING_5M=1`. See [ladder.md](ladder.md#reviewer-cache) for the measurements and cost per million tokens.

For a task with a PR in a project with `repo`, review dispatch also reads the PR's inline review comments through `gh api repos/<repo>/pulls/<pr>/comments` and lists in a `## Revuto findings` section every top-level comment by a login named `revuto` or `revuto-<app>`, with or without `[bot]`, that GitHub still anchors to a line at the head: path, line, numeric inline comment id, body, marked `[P1]` or `[P2]` when the body carries that tag, and the id and resolution status of the latest reply by another login. A failed read refuses the dispatch. The reviewer's `spawn` event records them as `revuto: [{ "id", "path", "line", "severity", "commit", "url", "reply", "resolved", "changed" }]`, where `severity` is `P1`, `P2` or null and `commit` is the comment's original commit. `resolved` is true only when the latest non-revuto reply has `[resolved]` as its entire first line, case-insensitively; an explanation may follow on later lines. Ordinary discussion, quoted markers and later replies without the marker do not resolve the finding. `changed` records whether, at dispatch, `git --literal-pathspecs diff <commit> <sha> -- <path>` named the literal file for a `[P1]`/`[P2]` without such a resolution (false when it cannot be read). `evidence --type review --ok` at that sha and revision is refused, naming each comment, while a recorded `[P1]`/`[P2]` has neither `resolved: true` nor `changed: true`. The refusal tells the reviewer that the worker or the orchestrator resolves a finding with `ci resolve COMMENT_ID --reason TEXT`, which posts the `[resolved]` reply and records a `ci resolve` event `{ "comment", "pr", "reply", "sha", "revision" }`. Workers find COMMENT_ID next to path:line as `(comment ID)` in `ci checks`, submit reports and rework prompts. Rework worker dispatch, including dry runs and resumed attempts, reads current open inline findings before locking state and appends their ids, locations and bodies alongside the failed review evidence; a failed read refuses dispatch unless the agent's gh policy denies gh api, in which case the prompt reports that the findings are unavailable and dispatch continues. The saved dispatch snapshot retains the findings or their policy-denial notice when a fallback route rebuilds the worker prompt. Older snapshots without `resolved` cannot use a reply id alone to clear the finding. The check reads only the state, so brokered evidence, which runs no git, gets the same answer. `--fail` is always accepted.

Reviewers use the supplied tests, clean and CI results. They do not re-run the full suite unless they change something in a scratch checkout to probe a specific concern, and run only the affected tests for that probe. The submitted worktree stays read-only. Packets over 12,000 characters put the diff in a context file; the user prompt still carries task context, gate results and revuto findings. This bounds diff arguments, not the static system text or unusually large gate receipts. Software waivers requested in the same accept remain atomic with acceptance and cannot authorize a review spawn; use audited software gate evidence or an owner accept that also waives review.

Review packets include `## Review scope`. When the latest eligible independent verdict at the current revision failed, dispatch checks its blockers and supplies only the diff from its reviewed SHA. Rework can carry this scope across its revision bump: the latest rework event must end at the current revision, and the latest eligible review at its rejected `sha` and `previous_revision` must have failed. A brief, acceptance, dependency, needs or kind edit after the review resets the scope; rework without a matching failure stays full. The review evidence event must be present in the audit trail. Unrelated older reviews and ineligible verdicts cannot narrow dispatch.

The review `spawn` event adds `review_scope: {sha, revision, agent, summary, ref, reason, findings}`, with the prior review's identity and blockers, linked comment, rework request and recorded `findings` (`null` when the review recorded none), or `null` for full review. It also adds `review_floor: {sha, revision, agent, review_rung, review_level}`, the failed review that set the re-review's rung floor with that review's own recorded rung and level, or `null` when the review follows no failed review. A fallback full re-review has `review_scope: null` and a `review_floor`. The same scope appears in the packet and in short prompts referencing large packets. Scope changes during diff preparation refuse dispatch under the lock. The event also records `review_level`, the complexity level selection computed. The reviewed SHA must still exist, be an ancestor of the submitted head and contain the current merge base with the project base to narrow the diff. A missing reviewed SHA, rewritten history or base update gets a full review from the project base's merge base, excluding upstream changes. That fallback packet still names the failed review with its summary, comment, findings and rework request, and asks the reviewer to confirm each prior blocker. A narrowed review takes its risk and size from both the new diff and the whole change. Narrowed or fallback, a re-review runs at or above the failed review's `review_rung` and `review_level`, except that `min_rung: "review"` starts it on the `review` rung until a review fails at the current head and revision. It reports whether each prior blocker is fixed, then reviews the new diff. A defect the new diff causes blocks wherever it shows, including in unchanged code. Other newly noticed findings on already reviewed work are follow-ups unless they are security or data-loss defects introduced by the change. The current revision still requires fresh software gate evidence and an independent review at its submitted SHA; the prior verdict supplies context only.

A gate passes when the latest eligible evidence of its type for the current revision, at a sha matching the submitted one, is ok. Shas match when one is a prefix of the other and the shorter has at least 7 characters. For `tests`, `clean`, `sources` and `ci`, evidence needs the matching gate `source` and an events.jsonl entry with `cmd === source`, the same task id and agent, and matching `type`, `source`, exact evidence `sha`, `ok`, `revision`, `commands` and optional `confirmed_failure` in `detail`. An ok entry also needs a non-empty commands list. Manual, forged and older unmarked entries are ignored, including later entries that would otherwise override a genuine pass or failure. For `review`, an entry counts only when its agent is `owner` or has a `spawn` event with `role: reviewer` for the task, at a sha matching the entry's and at the entry's revision, and is not the submitter. Recording review evidence needs no role, so a name no review dispatch started counts for nothing; its entry is kept, `task show` marks it `(does not count)` and the review gate names its agent. A waiver counts when its agent is `owner`; when the orchestrator recorded an operational waiver (`waive.review` for a capped or down reviewer); or when the orchestrator recorded it with `approved_by` naming an escalation the owner approved ([Authority](#authority)).

Local CI adds `receipt` to its evidence and event detail. Both copies must match exactly:

```json
{
  "variant": "default",
  "command": ["python3", "tools/check.py"],
  "timeout": 120,
  "exit": 0,
  "signal": null,
  "head_sha": "<full submitted commit SHA>",
  "base_sha": "<selected base commit SHA>",
  "tree_hash": "<Git tree object ID of base merged with head>",
  "source_digest": "<SHA-256 of git ls-tree -r -z TREE output>",
  "merge_sha": "<commit of tree_hash with parents base_sha and head_sha>",
  "duration_ms": 1000
}
```

`source_digest` is a SHA-256 restatement of the tree for consumers that do not trust SHA-1, computed from its tracked paths, modes and object IDs, including source blob IDs. It is fully determined by `tree_hash`. The runner computes both identities before execution; it refuses tracked-source changes or a merge result that changes while the command runs. The command runs in a temporary worktree checked out clean at `merge_sha`, a commit of the merged tree whose parents are `base_sha` and `head_sha`, so a check that requires a clean tree passes. Its author and committer are `Tower Crane <tower-crane@localhost>` at the newer parent's commit time, so the same base and head give the same commit. No ref names it and nothing is pushed. A failing execution still records its exit, signal and duration.

With `ci.local` configured, an ok CI gate needs a local receipt with exit 0, no signal, nonnegative duration, the resolved argv and timeout, and a matching successful command receipt. The receipt's `variant` is `default`, `kind:KIND` or `task:ID`, matching the selected default, kind mapping or task override. A different or missing variant fails even when argv and timeout are identical. The full head SHA, tree hash and source digest must match the current submitted head merged with the selected base. The latest audited evidence is checked by acceptance, merge and task gate reports. A different head fails even if the tree is identical; a changed merge result fails even if the head is unchanged. A base move with the same merged tree and source digest preserves the receipt for acceptance and task gate reports, including when a lower dependency lands with changes the checked head already contains. Changing the resolved variant, command or timeout, or switching CI modes, invalidates its earlier pass. Changing an unselected mapping has no effect. Invalid local policy or task overrides fail closed. Base selection follows dispatch using existing local and origin refs without fetching during `check ci`, acceptance or task reports; metadata queries each have a 60 s deadline. The origin/base pin is a separate merge precondition. Before merge validates a local receipt, it fetches origin's base with a 60 s timeout and requires that tip to equal the receipt's `base_sha`. Fetch failures and moved bases refuse before GitHub merge; a moved base needs `tower-crane check ci ID` again. Without origin, the local base is used. Once the latest audited merge evidence for the current head and revision is successful, reports retain the recorded CI result without validating it against later base changes, and repeated merges skip the fetch. An unaudited merge marker cannot bypass validation. No hosted API is needed for local CI. The existing hosted CI rules apply when local CI is absent.

Resubmitting replaces `sha` without deleting evidence. It preserves `revision` unless the probe list changes at the same commit, when the revision advances to retire the old declaration and its gate runs. Evidence at the older head stays in the audit trail and no longer counts for the new head. Resubmitting the same sha preserves gates only within the same revision and, for tests, with the same declared probes; rework or a changed brief requires fresh evidence. The task remains `submitted`, with no claim lease; omitted branch and PR values stay unchanged. When `--branch` or `--pr` is supplied and the task already has a PR, `submit` reads its state and head branch with `gh pr view`. An open PR blocks a different PR number or a different submitted branch. If the old PR is closed or merged and the PR number changes, `submit` reads the new PR's head branch, uses it when `--branch` is omitted, and checks a supplied branch against it. Failed or unreadable lookups refuse without changing state. Accepted tasks must go through `rework` and a new claim before submission, and that claimant becomes the new `submitted_by` agent.

A submitted head can move at any time until acceptance or rework. Reviewers must pin `evidence --sha` to the commit they reviewed so a result recorded after a resubmission still belongs to the older head. After rework, a fresh worker needs a claim. A session resume through `spawn` restores the submitting worker's claim under the dispatch lock, keeping its agent and original `since` with a new default lease and `from: rework`. An existing live claim by that worker stays unchanged. If an isolated Codex session is missing while the prior worker holds the rework claim, `spawn` transfers the claim to the fresh worker with a new default lease and records `took_over_from` in the claim event. An expired claim must pass readiness and worker-limit checks before transfer. If preparation or launch fails before a child pid is created, `spawn` restores the prior claim and records `rollback: true` in a claim event. A worker spawn on Windows or a host without `/bin/sh` commits its lease before launch. When that launch fails, it restores a prior claim the same way; for a task with no prior claim it records a `release` event with `rollback: true` instead. Another claimant cannot inherit the session.

The `rework` event records `previous_revision`, the originating rejected revision, and `revision`, the new revision. `rework --from-review` also records `from_review: true`. A repeated stack refresh while rework is pending advances `revision` but keeps that same `previous_revision`, including when a worker has claimed the rework. A new submission ends the pending rework; a later rejection records that submission's revision. Worker rework prompts include failed independent reviews at the originating revision and the reworked sha, including when later brief or acceptance edits advance the task again. Those reviews remain ineligible for the new revision's gates.

A rework that automation made for a failed `tests`, `clean` or `ci` gate also records `gate`, the gate that failed. Only such a rework can be undone by `gates retry ID --from-rework`, which records a `restore` event with `sha`, `gate`, the `rework` event id, `from_revision` (the rework revision) and `revision` (the restored one, the rework's `previous_revision`). The task goes back to submitted at the revision it was submitted at. Gate evidence is keyed to head and revision because a revision is a round of judgment: acceptance, dependencies, brief or a rework verdict. An automatic gate rework at an unchanged head opened a round nobody worked, so restoring the earlier revision keeps all its evidence at that head (passing gates and any review) valid by the same key, without copying receipts. The restored revision's failed gates then run again. `from_revision` is retired: every later bump (rework, acceptance, dependencies, needs, brief, stack refresh, quality climb, hotfix) takes the next number above both the current revision and every retired one, so evidence recorded at a retired revision never counts again. A light re-review treats the revision before the current one as the next unretired number down. A reviewer whose dispatch a later rework stopped counts neither as a held review nor toward `REVIEW_EXIT_CAP`: its revision returns only through a restore. A `task update --kind` after the rework does not bump the revision but changes which gates and review apply, so it also blocks the restore. Retired numbers hold only once every writer runs this version: a `wait` host or CLI started before it still bumps by one and can land on a retired number, where gate evidence recorded during the abandoned round (such as a `check ci` run to confirm the fix) would count again at the same head. Restart wait hosts after upgrading.

`merge` checks these gates again, for the current revision, before it merges.

The default method is squash. Commit text defaults to the task's `title` and `acceptance.join('\n')`; `merge ID --subject S --body B` overrides either independently without changing task state. Empty bodies and multiline text are preserved. Method `merge` uses the same text options; `rebase` omits them and refuses explicit text overrides. The command verifies the PR head matches the accepted SHA and always passes GitHub's full head SHA as `--match-head-commit`, including admin merges and merges that retain the branch. Merge command receipts record the selected text and options. When GitHub refuses `gh pr merge` for an unrecorded stack member, a squash or merge falls back to the asynchronous merge request (`merge_method` set to the squash or merge method, `expected_head_sha`, `commit_title` and `commit_message`); its receipts are the refused `gh pr merge` and the request, and the merge evidence records the merge commit. After confirmation the fallback deletes the head branch unless `merge.keep_branch` is set, and refuses when `merge.admin` is set, since the request has no admin option.

The ordinary merge gate refuses cross-repository PRs and a `baseRefName` different from the task's target base (`stack.base` or the project base). In unstacked fallback, every lower task must have merged before the project base becomes the target. Only a PR still targeting its recorded dependency branch may be retargeted, followed by a fresh state, head, repository and base check. Other wrong bases are refused without a PR write. Already-merged confirmation checks the base too, on the plain path and for every stack member, where the dependency branch or the project base is accepted. Summaries name GitHub's reported base; a merge confirmed into another base records failed evidence with the merge commit and asks for inspection of what landed.

For a stack member attempted in the current invocation, final confirmation requires the project base and a successful asynchronous result. The recorded dependency base is accepted only for members confirmed already merged before this invocation's attempts that have no prior audited asynchronous POST for the same repository, PR and accepted head. The gate reads command receipts from all merge events, including attempts recorded under a higher stack task. These receipts bind retries to the project base independently of stack sync. An asynchronous failure stays in the failed stack result; the member gets no successful merge evidence, while valid lower merges retain their own successful evidence.

The standards profile may add gates. `--waive TYPE --reason TEXT` records an owner waiver as evidence; only an explicit `--agent owner` or `TOWER_CRANE_AGENT=owner` can waive. A waiver satisfies its gate. If the accept is still refused, the waiver is not recorded.

A worker declares measurements or kernel checks its sandbox cannot run with repeatable `submit --host-probe CMD`. The task's `host_probes` is an ordered array of non-blank command strings without NUL bytes. Each submission replaces the array; omitting the flag clears previous probes. The submit event records that array, and `task show` lists it. Changing probes at the same commit advances `revision` with the existing retired-revision allocator. This invalidates all earlier gate evidence, including failures, and prevents a new check from joining an old probe run or automation from reworking the new declaration on its old verdict. An unchanged normalized list keeps the revision; a different sha already invalidates old runs and needs no extra revision. Commit probe scripts and say in the PR body which host results are pending; no decision is needed for these checks or cleanup. Leave cleanup to the clean gate.

After the head suite and host-only runs pass, the tests gate runs probes in order on the host, from the root of its detached scratch worktree at the submitted sha, before proof reversion. Probes take no input (stdin is closed), receive `tests.host_env` as host-only tests do, and run in gate containment scopes with the gate resource caps and a separate `gates.host_probe_timeout_min` budget each. None mode still runs declared probes, without a test command. Probes do not run at the base or during reverted proof. The first failed probe stops the remaining probes and reports its redacted output. Normal nonzero exits and timeouts fail the tests gate with `worker_failure: true`, returning through rework. Non-timeout signal kills, memory or PID cap interruptions and wait shutdown instead record `runner_crash: true`, including aborts without a reported signal on Windows. Automation retries these once at the head before rework, as it does for an interrupted suite; resource-cap diagnostics are retained. Timeout termination stops the process tree through the existing gate runner.

Tests evidence and its audit event include `receipt.host_probes`, also supplied in the reviewer prompt's gate results. Each executed probe records `{command, command_sha256, status, signal, timed_out, duration_ms, timeout_min, output_tail}`. Status is its exit code or null when signaled, duration is milliseconds including termination, and output is cleaned and redacted like test output before being bounded to the last 40 lines and 8192 characters. The command hash binds the receipt to the declared command without depending on its redacted display text. Declared probes that have not run leave an empty array in a failed head-suite receipt.

### Worker-written code and config outside the sandbox

The tests and clean gates run code the worker wrote. The tests gate's pinned command runs the submitted commit's tests, build files and scripts; its declared host probes run worker-written commands at the same trust level. The clean gate's pinned cleanup tool is the owner's, but it reads the submitted checkout, and a tool that loads config or plugins from it (a linter config, for one) runs the worker's code. Both run outside any sandbox, as the user who ran `check` or `accept` (for a reaction in a dispatch supervisor, the dispatcher), with that process's environment (`HOME`, `PATH`, `GH_TOKEN` and any other credentials it holds), in a temporary detached worktree of the commit under `TOWER_CRANE_TMP`, then `gates.tmp_root`, then `tc/g` under the user's cache directory. The only changes to that environment are `TMPDIR`, `TMP` and `TEMP`, which point at a scratch directory beside the worktree, and a set `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`, which moves into that directory. The cleanup tool also gets the git environment below, so the git it runs in that worktree takes no command from the repository's config. The tests command gets no git environment, since it runs the worker's code anyway. Local CI runs its command the same way as the tests gate. Review what a task changes before running its gates where that environment matters.

A sandboxed worker may write the repository's git directory (`writeOutside: git`), which holds its config, hooks and `info/attributes`. Every git command the CLI and gates start themselves (worktree, spawn preparation, submit scope, the gates' checkouts, diffs and fetches), every git that `gh` starts for them (`gh stack sync`, `checkout`, `link` and `merge`, `gh pr` calls) and every git the pinned cleanup tool starts takes command-running settings only from the system, global and command scopes, through `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` entries appended after any the caller already set:

- `core.hooksPath` is the trusted value when it is absolute or starts with `~/`, otherwise the null device, so the repository's `hooks/` directory and a hooks path the repository names never run.
- `core.fsmonitor` is `false`. `core.sshCommand`, `core.askPass`, `core.alternateRefsCommand`, `diff.external` and origin's `uploadpack` and `receivepack` take their trusted value, or a default that names no repository command (`ssh`, `git-upload-pack`, `git-receive-pack`, empty). Git reads `GIT_SSH` and `SSH_ASKPASS` only when `core.sshCommand` and `core.askPass` are unset, so the defaults carry them: `core.sshCommand` defaults to the caller's `GIT_SSH`, shell-quoted, when it is set, and `core.askPass` to the caller's `SSH_ASKPASS`. `core.pager` takes its trusted value, or the caller's `PAGER`, or `less`, which is the order git reads them in after the caller's `GIT_PAGER`. `protocol.ext.allow` and `protocol.fd.allow` are `never`; fetches do not recurse into submodules. `maintenance.auto` is `false` whatever its scope, since the `gc --auto` that fetch, commit, merge and rebase start runs prune and repack, which run `gc.recentObjectsHook`.
- `GIT_NO_REPLACE_OBJECTS` is `1`, so replace refs in the common directory a worker writes do not change the history and objects these commands see.
- `GIT_PROTOCOL_FROM_USER` is `0`, since no URL these commands use is typed by the user, so a protocol git allows only for a typed URL (any remote helper beyond `http`, `https`, `git` and `ssh`) is refused unless trusted config allows it. `protocol.file.allow` is the trusted value, or the trusted `protocol.allow`, or `always`, so local remotes keep working as before; git makes `file` user-only for the submodules a clone recurses into, and these fetches recurse into none.
- `GIT_PROXY_COMMAND` is the caller's value or empty, which overrides all `core.gitProxy` entries. Git proxy commands must come from that environment variable because config uses the first matching entry.
- The credential helper list is cleared, then refilled with the trusted `credential.helper` and `credential.<url>.helper` entries in their order.
- Each filter (`clean`, `smudge`, `process`), diff driver (`command`, `textconv`), merge driver, `remote.<name>.uploadpack` or `receivepack`, `gpg.program`, `gpg.<format>.program`, `gpg.ssh.defaultKeyCommand`, `alias.<name>` and `pager.<cmd>` key the repository's own config files set takes the trusted value, or is emptied so git refuses to run it (an empty `pager.<cmd>` is false); such a filter is not `required`. A trusted filter reads the same config, so git-lfs's command keys are treated the same way: `lfs.customtransfer.<name>.path` and `args`, `lfs.standalonetransferagent` and `lfs.<url>.standalonetransferagent`, and `lfs.extension.<name>.clean` and `smudge`, matched without regard to case. A transfer or extension the repository adds then fails instead of running; `.lfsconfig` honors none of these keys. A `protocol.<name>.allow` or `protocol.allow` the repository sets takes the trusted value, or the policy git would apply without it: the trusted `protocol.allow`, then `always` for `http`, `https`, `git`, `ssh` and `file` and `user` for any other. Aliases are covered because git runs `alias.remote-<name>` for a `<name>::` URL or `remote.<r>.vcs` when no `git-remote-<name>` program exists. Those files are the shared `config`, the `config.worktree` of the worktree git runs in and every file they include through `include.path` or `includeIf`, whatever its condition, so a `gitdir:`, `onbranch:` or `hasconfig:` include that matches only in a task or gate worktree is covered too.

Git calls that read config and refs but no object, index or hook run without the scan: `git rev-parse` asking only where the repository is (`--git-dir`, `--git-common-dir`, `--absolute-git-dir`, `--show-toplevel`, `--is-inside-work-tree`, `--is-bare-repository`, `--path-format`), `git worktree list`, `prune`, `lock` and `unlock`, and `git remote get-url`.

Git clears the command scope for a push to a repository on this machine, so that repository's own config and hooks run. The CLI's one push, the generated-file repair, goes to `origin` by name after its push URL is checked against the PR (see `generated merge` below), so a repository `pushInsteadOf` cannot send it elsewhere. The pushes `gh stack sync` makes go to whatever origin URL the repository config names, and nothing here checks that URL.

A process reuses a scan for the same checkout and caller environment while every file it read holds the same bytes, and each directory it checked keeps its mode: the checkout's `.git`, the git directory's `HEAD`, `commondir`, `objects` and `refs`, the shared `config`, `config.worktree`, each included file and the trusted config files the scan took entries from. They are read again before each command, and any change to them scans again. A file with no include that is empty, or that git's own listing for the checkout already read, takes its keys from that listing; any other file is parsed from the recorded bytes, one git process each. A checkout with no `.git` of its own, one git resolves elsewhere, or an environment that sets `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE` or `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY` is scanned for every command.

Git and gh also refuse to start while the common directory of the repository they run in holds a `commondir` file, which git never writes there and which would point git, and Tower Crane's state lookup, at another repository ([ladder.md](ladder.md#the-common-directorys-commondir)). The check finds the git directory as git does (`GIT_DIR`, else the nearest `.git` or bare repository) and covers the calls that skip the scan. Only a worktree admin directory, one holding `gitdir` and no `objects/`, may name a common directory, and only one that holds it at `worktrees/<id>` (directly, or as `.git-admin/<name>` through a link there); any other `commondir` is refused. Spawn refuses a worktree whose `.git` file names a git directory that is not such an admin directory of the repository, whose `commondir` names its common directory, before granting it to a sandbox. Where git's answer decides a path (`--git-common-dir` for the state directory and the gates' worktree lock, `--git-dir` for a spawn's git directories), it must match that location, so a file that appears between the check and git's lookup is refused too. The supervisor removes a `commondir` in the common directory and records at most one `commondir removed` event per sample interval, with `agent`, `pid`, `path`, `named` (what the file last named) and `count`.

The config scan must complete successfully before git or gh starts. An error, timeout or output buffer overflow refuses the command rather than using a partial driver list, and so does a repository include whose path starts with `~user/` or `%(prefix)/`, which the scan does not resolve. Named driver keys are checked against the config files just before each command, so a key a worker adds between that read and git's own can still run. The fixed keys above have no such gap. Claude Code's sandbox mounts the git directory's `config`, `config.worktree` and `hooks/` read-only, which closes the gap for claude workers. The codex sandbox rules grant the whole git directory, so a codex worker can still add a key. The worker's own git and gh, through their shims, keep the repository's config.

### What the git and gh shims guard

Every spawned agent finds its `git` and `gh` shims first on `PATH`: claude, opencode, agy, pi and `command` agents in `homes/<agent>/bin/`, a sandboxed codex agent in `homes/<agent>/home/.tower-crane-shims/bin/`, which its sandbox shows read-only ([ladder.md](ladder.md#agent-files-and-homes)). Until 2026-10-14 a sandboxed codex agent's sandbox hid its shims, so `git` and `gh` were the host's and none of the shim policy applied to codex agents.

The shims hold an agent that runs `git` and `gh` by name, and the tools that look them up on `PATH`, to its role's push and gh policy. They do not hold an agent that means to get past them: `/usr/bin/git` and `/usr/bin/gh` are readable and run in both sandboxes. What holds such an agent is that it has nothing to push or call GitHub with.

**A sandboxed agent holds no GitHub credential.** Claude and codex agents whose agent file says `sandbox: true` (worker, researcher, reviewer, small) get no `GH_TOKEN`, `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN` or `GITHUB_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`: spawn does not ask the keyring for them, and the supervisor removes them from the agent's environment on every launch, retry and fallback, whether the dispatcher's environment, the project or rung `env` or an `env_file` sets them. The rendered git config clears every credential helper and adds none ([ladder.md: git config](ladder.md#home-reads)). The sandbox hides the user's home (the gh config, `~/.git-credentials`, `~/.ssh`) and `/run` (the keyring's session bus, the SSH agent). `test/live-sandbox.test.js` checks this in the real claude and codex sandboxes, spawned by a user whose gh is logged in: no token variable; no file the agent can read (its `HOME`, the state directory, its git config, every `/proc/<pid>/environ` it sees) holds the user's token; `git credential fill` answers nothing for GitHub; `/usr/bin/git push` over https fails for lack of credentials; the SSH agent socket does not connect.

**Pushes, fetches from origin and gh go through the broker.** The agent's shims send them to its [state broker](ladder.md#state-broker), which runs outside the sandbox, checks them again there, and runs them with the user's own login (`lib/github-broker.js`).

- `git push` of the task branch to `origin`, from the task's repository, sends only the commit the agent names. The broker loads the role's agent file and refuses a role without `gitPush: branch`. The destination is always `refs/heads/<task branch>`, never the project's base branch, and the request has no field that could name another. The URL is `origin`'s, read from the repository's config file without includes or URL rewriting, and it must be the project's recorded GitHub repository (https or ssh), or a path on this machine for a project that records none. The broker opens nothing in the task repository's object store: the agent can write it, git does not re-hash a loose object when it reads one, and an alternates entry or a link there would point the broker, which reads with the user's permissions, at a repository the sandbox hides. Instead the broker's own bare repository, `push/cache.git` in the state directory, fetches the remote's branches, and the broker sends the shim the remote's branch heads (`{need: {have}}`). The shim builds a thin pack of the commit in the sandbox (`git pack-objects --revs --thin`, bounded by the heads it holds) and sends it on the same connection in length-prefixed frames, ended by an empty frame, and the broker indexes it with `index-pack --strict --fix-thin`, which computes each object's id from its content, then requires every object of the commit the remote lacks to be present. A commit the cache already holds whole needs no pack. The pack goes into a quarantine object directory beside the cache, as receive-pack does, and moves into the cache only after every check passes, so a refused push leaves nothing. A pack over 2 GiB (GitHub's push limit) is refused, and so is one that stalls for 10 minutes; pack-objects writes nothing while it searches for deltas, which takes minutes on a large history. When the broker answers before it read the whole pack, the shim stops its pack-objects and exits with the answer. The shim never ends its side of the connection to mark the end of the pack, since a Windows named pipe has no half close; any end of the agent's side is a hang-up, and the broker stops what it runs for that agent, including its checks and its push after the whole pack arrived. Every release frames the pack, and a spawn's shims and broker run one copy of the tool (`homes/<agent>/tool/`), so an upgrade during a run does not split them. A shim from a snapshot before framed packs (T291) sends a bare pack: the broker sees its `PACK` signature where the first frame's length belongs, refuses the push and says the shim and broker come from different snapshots. The cache takes the remote's object format (from `ls-remote`, or the commit id's length for an empty remote) and is made again when its format differs. One push at a time uses it, under the `push/cache.lock` directory, which holds one marker per holder; a dead holder's marker is removed by name, so two waiters never both take the lock. Lock files and temporary packs a killed git left, and quarantines a killed broker left, are removed before each push. The broker refuses the push when a commit the remote lacks changes a file under `.github/workflows/` or `.github/actions/`, or the `action.yml` or `action.yaml` of a local action a workflow in the pushed commit runs (`uses: ./path`), that the task's title, acceptance or brief does not name (a merge counts only the files it changed against every parent). These checks and the push read only the cache. git runs without the dispatcher's `GIT_TRACE*` and `GIT_CURL_VERBOSE` variables, since the push's stderr reaches the agent. It then pushes without force (`--no-verify`, no tags, no submodules), so a rewritten history is refused as a non-fast-forward. Output streams back as it comes, with git's progress. The broker's `ls-remote`, fetch and push talk to the remote under the cache lock, so each runs with `--progress` where git has it and is stopped, with the ssh or remote helper it started, after 10 minutes without output or an hour in all, the limits of the agent's pack. A moving transfer prints about once a second; the 10 minutes cover the remote's checks of a push and the connectivity check and gc a fetch runs. A remote that stops answering, as over a network that drops without a reset, then fails the push or fetch with the reason and frees the lock for the project's other agents. The shim refuses a force, deletion, wildcard or other-branch refspec before it asks, and after a push records `refs/remotes/origin/<branch>` (and the upstream with `-u`, where the config is writable).
- `git fetch origin` (and `git fetch` whose current branch fetches from origin) from the task's repository goes to the broker when origin's one URL, read from the config without URL rewriting, is the project's recorded GitHub repository (https or ssh). git fetches any other origin, a path on this machine included, in the sandbox as before. Inside the sandbox it cannot work: there is no credential, the SSH agent's socket is under the hidden `/run`, and in a user namespace ssh refuses `/etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/*` because root-owned files appear owned by nobody (`Bad owner or permissions`). The broker checks origin's URL again, from the repository's config file without includes or URL rewriting, and fetches only from the recorded GitHub repository: a project that records none, or a local path, gets no brokered fetch, since the broker reads with the user's permissions and a path could name a repository the sandbox hides. The broker brings `push/cache.git` up to date under the cache lock, as for a push, and answers with the branches the task may fetch: the project base, the base its pull request targets when stacked, and the task branch, or those of them the agent names; any other branch is refused. It sends their heads (`{refs}`, null for a branch origin lacks), then a thin pack of their commits (`pack-objects --revs --thin`) bounded by the commits the agent says it holds that the cache has, as base64 `{pack}` lines that wait for the connection to drain. An agent that reads none of it for 10 minutes, or has not read it all after an hour, ends the fetch and frees the cache lock; a push's pack has the same hour cap, and the broker's own fetch from the remote the limits above. The shim indexes the pack into the task repository with `index-pack --stdin --fix-thin`, where git hashes every object, checks that each head arrived whole, and updates `refs/remotes/origin/<branch>` and `FETCH_HEAD`. When index-pack fails (a read-only git directory, a full disk), the shim drops the rest of the pack, waits for the broker's answer and exits 1 with index-pack's error, leaving origin's branches as they were. It takes branch names, `refs/heads/B` and `+refs/heads/B:refs/remotes/origin/B`, and `-q`, `-v`, `-p` (prunes a fetchable branch origin lacks), `-n`/`--no-tags`, `--[no-]progress` and `--no-write-fetch-head`; it refuses other refspecs and options, such as tags, `--unshallow` or `--all`. `git pull` from such an origin is refused with the way that works: fetch, then merge `origin/<branch>`. The same request runs over every broker transport: a Unix socket for claude, loopback TCP for codex, a named pipe on Windows. macOS and Windows have no user-namespace owner remapping, but the agent holds no credential there either, so fetch goes through the broker on every platform.
- A push to a path on this machine, such as a test fixture's bare repository, still runs in the sandbox, which decides what it can write. A push from elsewhere to another machine is refused: the agent has no credential for it.
- `gh` runs on the host in the project's recorded repository (the broker refuses gh for a project that records none), with no local repository for gh to read and an environment of only what gh needs: `PATH`, `HOME`, the locale, the XDG and gh config directories, the session bus its keyring answers on and the proxy settings. gh's config directory (`GH_CONFIG_DIR`, else `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/gh`, else `~/.config/gh`) must not resolve inside the state directory (which holds the agent homes), the agent's worktree or the task repository's git directory; the broker refuses gh otherwise. A token the dispatcher's environment holds goes in too, and then `--jq` is refused, since gh's jq reads the environment. The broker parses the arguments with gh's own flag table (`lib/gh-flags.json`, regenerated by `node scripts/gh-flags.js`; a flag it does not list is refused) and checks the role's gh policy; `gh search` and `gh status`, which read across every repository the user sees, are not brokered. It refuses flags that act on the host (`--web`, `--editor`, `--recover`, `--attach`, `auth status --show-token`) and any flag that reads a file by path, except `--body-file`, whose file the shim reads in the sandbox and sends as stdin. `-R` must name the project's repository and a URL argument is refused. `pr create` comes from the task branch only. A command that changes a pull request (`pr edit`, `pr comment`, `pr review`, `pr ready`, `pr close`, `pr reopen`, `pr merge`, as the role allows) acts only on a pull request whose head is the task branch in the same repository; without a number it is the task branch's. When the agent hangs up, as `timeout ... gh pr checks --watch` does, the broker stops the gh it started.

**Agents without an OS sandbox.** opencode, agy, pi and `command` agents run as the user, without a sandbox, so they can read whatever the user can: the gh config, the keyring through the session bus, `~/.ssh`. Spawn still hands them `GH_TOKEN` and their shims run `git` and `gh` directly. No token guarantee holds for them; it needs an OS sandbox. That is one reason a worker, reviewer or small rung on one of them is owner-required (`ladder.reach`, [Authority](#authority)).

**On GitHub.** The broker uses the user's own login, so what it can do is bounded by the checks above, not by the token's scopes. Keep a ruleset on the default branch as well, so a mistake in the broker or a human slip cannot reach it: require a pull request, allow only the merge method the project uses, and block force pushes and deletion, with no bypass actors. `agent-sh/tower-crane` has one since 2026-10-14 (ruleset `main: PRs only, no force push or deletion`, active on `~DEFAULT_BRANCH`: pull request required with squash merges only, `non_fast_forward` and `deletion` rules, no bypass actors; `gh api repos/agent-sh/tower-crane/rules/branches/main` lists the three rules). GitHub cannot scope a token to one branch, so the task-branch rule lives in the broker.

## decisions.json

```json
{
  "version": 1,
  "next": 2,
  "decisions": [
    {
      "id": "D1",
      "question": "Store idempotency keys in Redis or Postgres?",
      "options": ["redis", "postgres"],
      "recommendation": "postgres",
      "why": "keys must survive a Redis flush; volume is 30/s",
      "blocks": ["T3"],
      "answerers": [],
      "kind": "technical",
      "technical": true,
      "status": "open",
      "answer": null,
      "note": null,
      "asked_by": "worker-T3-1",
      "asked_at": "...",
      "answered_by": null,
      "answered_at": null,
      "answer_rule": null,
      "withdrawn_by": null,
      "withdrawn_at": null,
      "withdraw_reason": null
    }
  ]
}
```

A decision the engine opened for an orchestrator's owner-required change also has `approval_request: true`, `escalation: { "settings": [...], "change": {...} }`, options `["approve", "decline"]`, and, once the orchestrator used an approval or the owner made the matching change, `applied: { "at", "by" }` ([Authority](#authority)). One opened by a command also has `request: { "command", "flags", "pos" }`, the command as it was run; the Controls page applies an approved one the owner asked for there. When the orchestrator asks for the same change while the owner's Controls request is open, it waits on that decision, which records `repeat_by: "<agent>"`; the page then leaves the approval for that agent's repeat. The generated request and its `ask` event carry `approval_request: true`; only marked requests can be reused or applied. Older unmarked decisions cannot authorize a command. A tagged question (`ask --question Q --kind K --setting KEY`) records `escalation` with `change: null` for owner-required tags and is not technical. It is never a one-use approval.

A decision blocks only the tasks it lists. Everything else keeps running. `status` is `open`, `answered` or `withdrawn`. When options are listed, the answer must be one of them. An explicit owner identity can answer every decision. `answerers` lists agents the owner named with `decision delegate DID --answerers JSON`; the owner can replace the list or clear it with `[]`. `decision delegate` is owner-required: an orchestrator request opens an approval for the target decision and supplied fields, then its identical rerun applies it once. The shared audit names `decision.delegate`; closed decisions refuse before opening or consuming an approval. `kind` is what the asker says the question is, and every `ask --question` gives one: `technical`, `spend`, `credential`, `setting` or `crisis` (other values are refused, and a missing kind is a usage error). The question text is never read, so routing depends on the kind and the state alone. `credential` is only for a request that grants a credential or loosens a sandbox: the ask must also name one of `env`, `env_file`, `sandbox`, `sandbox.session_bus`, `scope`, `ladder.reach` or `ladder.web_mcp` with `--setting`, or it exits 2; a host or harness that fails is technical. A decision is owner-only when it escalates an owner-required setting, when its `ask` carries `--owner-required REASON` (another call only the owner makes; stored as `owner_required`), or when its kind is `spend`, `credential`, `setting` or `crisis`.

A worker's or reviewer's question (an identity that is neither the owner nor the orchestrator) that blocks a task whose latest tests evidence, at any revision, failed with `main_failure` (every failing name also fails on the base) goes to the orchestrator: it is stored with `kind: "technical"`, `technical: true`, no escalation and no `owner_required`, plus `routed: { to: "orchestrator", task, base, names, asked_kind, dropped?, owner_required? }` keeping what the asker gave. No owner grant fixes such a failure: the gate keeps the task submitted and reruns when main moves. A `spend` or `crisis` question, or one that names an owner-required setting other than a `waive.*` gate waiver, keeps its route. The `ask` event carries `routed`, and the command prints the id with the route.

`crisis` is the one class that reaches the owner unasked: host stability (OOM, fork storm, disk full), data loss or state corruption, a security incident (a leaked secret, a sandbox escape), or a published artifact that is broken. `ask --kind crisis --crisis host|data|security|artifact` stores `crisis` with the class (only a crisis decision has it), is owner-only, and appends a `msg` event to `owner` in the same write. Everything else resolves through policy, the orchestrator or automation. `status` and `inbox` end with one owner line: `owner: crisis D<n> (<class>): <question>` while a crisis is open; otherwise `owner: no crisis; waiting when convenient: <ids>` naming open decisions the orchestrator cannot answer and open tasks whose hold is owner-required; otherwise `owner: nothing needed`. Their JSON carries it as `owner: { line, crisis, waiting }`. A decision the engine opens (an owner-required change, or an exhausted escalation range) has no `kind` and is owner-only. `technical` is stored at ask time: true only for a `technical` kind with no escalation and no reason. Open decisions keep their stored value, so an open technical decision is answerable under the default without re-asking. A decision with no `kind` (opened before kinds were stored) is technical on upgrade with no owner step, unless an `escalation` or an `owner_required` reason keeps it with the owner; its stored flag is not read. The owner sets `technical` through `decision delegate` (`--technical false` keeps a decision with the owner; on a decision with no kind it stores `owner_required` as `the owner keeps it with the owner`, which `--technical true` clears). The orchestrator can answer only a decision that has no owner-only reason and is technical: it has no `kind`, or its `technical` is true (the owner's lift sets it, whatever the kind). This also needs `decision_delegation.orchestrator_technical` not to be false. Owner-only decisions refuse every non-owner answer. The owner's `decision delegate DID --technical true` lifts a spend, credential or setting kind and an `--owner-required` reason (its stored `owner_required` becomes `null`), but never an escalated setting; a decision that keeps an owner-only kind or reason refuses `--answerers`. An answer stores `answered_by` and `answer_rule`; older decisions default to no named answerers, not technical, and no answer rule.

A withdrawal sets `status` to `withdrawn` while no answer exists. Its `withdrawn_by` is the verified identity that opened the decision (`asked_by`) or `owner`, or `engine` for a moot decision (below); `withdrawn_at` and a non-empty `withdraw_reason` are required with it. The `decision withdraw` event records `detail.decision`, `detail.reason`, `detail.blocks` and `detail.withdrawn_by`, and maps to `decision-withdrawn`. A withdrawn decision no longer blocks its tasks, is off the open list, and cannot be answered, delegated or withdrawn again; it stays in `decisions.json` and in event history. Each blocked task gets a note with the reason.

An open decision closes itself as moot in the write that closes the last task it waits on: every task it `blocks`, and for an approval request the task its `change` names as `task`, `accept` or `release`, is `accepted` or `cancelled` (a superseded task is cancelled). A re-record request (`task.rerecord_review`) acts on cancelled tasks and a `publish` request waits for its task to be accepted, so neither names one. Any write does it, whoever runs it. The decision records `status: "withdrawn"`, `withdrawn_by: "engine"`, `moot: true` and a `withdraw_reason` naming each task and why, such as `moot: T3 accepted, T4 superseded by T5`; its `decision withdraw` event, under the agent whose write closed it, carries the same reason with `withdrawn_by: "engine"` and `moot: true`. No task note is added: every task it named is closed. Only a write that closes one of those tasks makes the decision moot: a decision asked about tasks that were all closed already, such as a publish request for an accepted release task, stays open until it is answered or withdrawn. A crisis decision (`kind: "crisis"`) never becomes moot; the host or data trouble it reports outlives its tasks. A decision that blocks nothing and requests no task change never becomes moot this way.

Answer rules are `owner`, `owner-named-agent` and `owner-technical-delegation`. The `answer` event records the caller in `agent` and `detail.answered_by`, and the rule in `detail.answer_rule`. Refused answers write no event. Answering adds a note with the answer to every task the decision blocked. Optional `notes` contains `{ "at", "agent", "text" }` comments added with `decision note`, including serve's owner form.

The orchestrator's technical rule takes precedence over `answerers`, including for a generated identity with a recorded orchestrator role. Listing that identity cannot authorize a nontechnical answer or one without project delegation. A sandboxed agent may answer through its broker only when the owner named its bound identity for that decision; successful brokered answer events retain the bound identity and carry `via: broker`.

## events.jsonl

Long-lived wait hosts, spawn supervisors and board servers retain parsed history and a byte offset. They read only appended bytes, buffer incomplete lines until a newline arrives, go back to where a recorded cut left the log ([Durability and write order](#durability-and-write-order)), and reset on any other truncation or on file replacement. Reset rebuilds retained history from the beginning. A restore that also brings back a shorter `events.cuts.jsonl` makes a saved `OFFSET:CUTS` cursor whose `CUTS` is past the restored file's length exit 2 from `wait` and 400 from `GET /api/messages`, as an offset past a restored log does; restart those clients with a fresh cursor. A live reader whose log was replaced starts over with the restored length; one whose log was rewritten in place ignores cut records until `events.cuts.jsonl` grows past the length it had read. Active waits and board SSE do not wake for restored records, across as many reads as the restore takes. They keep the ids of the records they read since they started: after a reset a record with one of those ids is history, and so is history from before a reader's start cursor up to the line that ended at that cursor. Any other record wakes, whatever its writer's clock. A restore that lacks the start line, an older backup, leaves only time: records whose `at` is no later than the reader's start are history. Project, task and decision snapshots whose files are unchanged and whose event log has had nothing appended since they were read retain their validation and parsed values; mutations always load fresh writable copies.
